8 Agent Sandboxes

Lecture slides for this chapter

Purpose

Agent stack focus for this chapter.

Why must the limit on what an agent’s code can reach be enforced below the model rather than requested of it?

When an agent runs a shell command or a test script, what that code can touch is set by the environment it runs in, not by the tool description or the prompt that produced it. A command that passes every schema check can still read a credentials file, open a connection to an unapproved host, fork until the machine stalls, or rewrite the tests that will judge it. Model alignment and system-prompt instructions lower the chance of such a proposal but bound nothing, and a restriction enforced inside the supervisor’s own interpreter leaves reachable objects and inherited authority in place. The limit must instead be enforced beneath the executing process, where the operating system or a hypervisor mediates files, processes, network egress, and resource quotas regardless of what the code attempts. That confinement has a price. Every isolation envelope adds startup latency and memory, a workspace must persist across the turns of one task without leaking into the next, and a platform serving many tasks needs a reserve of warm environments that return to service only after a verified reset. An environment handed to the next tenant with a stray background process or a lingering credential turns one task’s compromise into another’s. Sandboxing turns a task’s authority into limits the host enforces on what code can read, change, send, and consume, and on how long it may do so, so that a destructive proposal can damage only what the task was granted.

Learning Objectives
  • Explain why containment must be enforced beneath the executing process, not inside the supervisor’s interpreter
  • Apply the capability admission check, attenuation rule, and credential brokering to bound what agent-run code can reach under a task grant
  • Select an execution envelope and its per-call or per-trajectory lifetime from reachable objects, system-call cost, and setup cost
  • Calculate copy-on-write provisioning and first-modification cost for task-local workspaces
  • Diagnose disclosure channels that remain open under an egress policy, including name resolution
  • Specify a reset contract over execution, storage, network, and device state before an instance is reused
  • Select a sandbox pool’s standby reserve and provisioning path by calculating whether memory residency or restore latency binds
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

8.1 The Reach of Admitted Code

Consider an adversarial variant of the connection-pool repair executed by the repair agent (Four lighthouse workloads). While inspecting a vendored dependency to verify how the library is configured, the coding agent reads its README.md file. Hidden near the bottom, inside an HTML comment stripped from rendered documentation but visible in raw source, lies a prompt injection addressed to automated assistants. It instructs the agent to upload host environment variables and cloud credentials to an external diagnostics endpoint before running any unit tests. Following these instructions, the model generates a run_shell command that reads ~/.aws/credentials and attempts to transmit the contents over an HTTP socket. The proposed invocation complies with the tool’s parameter schema. A naive agent runtime that checks only whether run_shell is an authorized tool admits the call immediately, despite the task granting neither read access to host credentials nor permission to disclose them.

Authority bounded beneath the model, limiting what code can change, read, and send.

The typed action contracts established in Structure, permission, and state checks check each proposed invocation’s structure, authority, and state preconditions before dispatch. Static contract validation, however, cannot solve the broader problem for a general-purpose command runner. Once an admitted subprocess begins execution, it can access every file descriptor, network socket, and system resource that the host process environment permits, regardless of what the tool description or prompt claimed. Securing an agent therefore requires sandboxing: projecting the task’s formal boundaries directly into the operating system environment where the admitted code runs.

Designing this execution environment demands balancing strict containment against the operational needs of real software tasks. For the connection-pool repair, the sandbox must provide a mutable workspace for source edits while mounting acceptance test suites as read-only volumes so the agent cannot tamper with evaluation criteria. Because a runaway test loop or fork bomb can exhaust host capacity without touching a forbidden file, the operating system must also cap CPU time, memory allocations, ephemeral storage, and process fork counts. The sandbox must also keep repository credentials off the guest filesystem by routing authenticated requests through external brokers. It must restrict outbound traffic to approved package registries and diagnostic services. Across a multi-turn trajectory, that environment must persist long enough for intermediate edits and compiler artifacts to survive from one step to the next, yet reset deterministically before another task arrives. Every boundary must hold even if the untrusted code gains administrative privileges inside its own environment, the worst case the design assumes. Every isolation mechanism also imposes startup latency and memory overhead that high-throughput agent platforms must provision.

Each of these requirements answers a question the README attack raises. Which inputs are hostile, and which components must stay correct for any limit to hold, is the threat model (section 8.2). Why the limit cannot live inside the supervisor’s own interpreter, where pruned names leave reachable objects, native libraries, and inherited authority in place, comes next (section 8.3). What the code should hold instead is a capability scoped to the task, with credentials kept outside the guest (the code running inside the boundary, together with the environment it sees) by a broker (section 8.4). An execution envelope, the enforced boundary around running code, imposes that scope. Which envelope fits among shared-kernel containers, user-space kernels, microVMs, and bytecode runtimes depends on the workload’s system calls, authority, and horizon (section 8.5). The remaining sections give the envelope a workspace that persists across turns without leaking between tenants (section 8.6) and close the outbound channels a confined process can still use (section 8.7). They then price the whole arrangement as a pool of environments that must be reset before reuse and sized for bursts of arrivals (section 8.8).

8.2 The Agent Threat Model

In the repair scenario, a single line planted in README.md turns a routine dependency check into an attempt to steal host credentials, while production vulnerabilities in shipped coding assistants demonstrate that sandbox escapes and command-injection failures are active operational risks rather than theoretical curiosities. A rigorous threat model for the repair task must therefore specify which inputs may be hostile, which failure modes the execution boundary must contain, and what resources the task legitimately requires from its environment.

The threat model must also fix how capable the hostile code is, and measured capability has moved quickly. Zhang et al. (2024) assemble 40 professional-level capture-the-flag tasks, each initialized in an environment where an agent runs commands and observes their output, and score whether the agent identifies the vulnerability and executes the exploit without intermediate hints. Figure 1 plots the unguided solve rate of each evaluated model against its release date.

The best unguided result among models released in 2024 was 17.5 percent; the latest reported result is 93 percent. That trend removes a comfortable assumption from the threat model. Admitted code cannot be treated as merely buggy, because the model proposing it is measured finding and exploiting weaknesses in exactly the kind of environment a sandbox provides. Every interface the guest can reach, from the filesystem and process table to the network stack and the host kernel, is therefore attack surface. Containment must hold against code written by a capable exploit author, even when that code arrives as an ordinary step of the repair.

8.2.1 Untrusted input and execution reach

The planted README payload is an instance of indirect prompt injection, in which attacker-controlled material encountered as task data attempts to redirect model execution (Greshake et al. 2023). Language models cannot reliably separate control instructions from untrusted data in their input context, and adversarial evaluation can bound the resulting attack success rate but never certify it as zero. While interface-level mechanisms such as message roles and provenance labels help structure conversational context, they do not enforce operating system protections. The sandbox runtime must maintain strict isolation boundaries even when the model faithfully obeys a hostile instruction. Unsafe proposals also arise routinely without an active adversary; an unintended command-line flag, a misspelled path, or a mistaken target directory produces identical operational damage. A production vulnerability in a commercial coding agent illustrates how quickly text-based admission checks collapse when decoupled from low-level isolation.

↪ Why a language model cannot separate control instructions from untrusted data in its input is developed in Prompt injection entry points.

↪ Why adversarial testing bounds but cannot zero the attack success rate is developed in Adaptive attack testing.

War Story 8.1: Gemini CLI allow-list bypass (2025)
Context: Google released the Gemini CLI coding agent on June 25, 2025, and on June 27 Tracebit reported that a repository could make it run commands the user never saw (Cox 2025). The CLI asked the user before running a shell command and let the user approve a command such as grep for later reuse.

Mechanism: The injected instructions sat inside the full text of the GNU General Public License in a repository’s README.md, where the model parsed them while a human reviewer was unlikely to notice them. They steered the agent to run an innocuous grep, which the user approved, followed by a chained command that began with grep and appended env | curl targeting a remote server. Because the allow-list check extracted only the root binary name with cursory validation, the chained command matched the approved grep rule. A long sequence of whitespace inside the command string pushed the appended payload off-screen in the interactive confirmation prompt.

Impact: The proof of concept silently exfiltrated the process’s environment variables, exposing sensitive credentials to the attacker’s server under the full authority of the user’s shell session. Google initially triaged the report at low priority and low severity (P2/S4) and raised both (to P1/S1) on July 23.

Fix: Version 0.1.14, released July 25, 2025, displays the complete command string and requires explicit approval for each additional binary invoked. Tracebit also recommended running agentic tools in dedicated sandboxing modes wherever possible.

Systems lesson: Textual validation of a command string functions merely as an admission filter at the prompt interface. Once admitted, an executing process is constrained only by the operating system environment hosting it. Because the agent executed directly within the developer’s interactive shell, a single approved command inherited all the authority of the user’s shell session. It could therefore read local environment variables, credentials, and configuration files and open network sockets over the host interface.

Cox, Sam. 2025. Code Execution Through Deception: Gemini AI CLI Hijack. Tracebit blog.
Willison, Simon. 2025. The Lethal Trifecta for AI Agents: Private Data, Untrusted Content, and External Communication. Personal blog.

Both the Gemini CLI incident and the repository repair scenario combine untrusted content that steers the proposal, sensitive local state the executing code can read, and a channel that can carry that state out. Willison named this conjunction the lethal trifecta for agents (Willison 2025), and The lethal trifecta develops it. A sandbox cannot remove the untrusted content, because reading a vendored README is often the task itself, but it can shrink what the code can read and block the routes out. Exfiltration, however, is not the only failure mode. Local source code may itself be proprietary, and destructive workspace corruption or runaway compute loops require no network connectivity at all.

Sandbox design therefore begins by cataloging protected assets alongside legitimate execution requirements. For the code repair task, the runtime must provide a task-local source tree, necessary toolchains, and writable scratch space to execute test suites. It must exclude the developer’s home directory, SSH keys, unrelated repositories, and neighboring tenant files. Permitted external communication, such as package downloads or artifact publishing, requires explicit, scoped endpoints rather than open internet access. These constraints belong in enforced system configuration, not in natural language instructions asking the model to remain within bounds.

Definition 8.1: Blast radius

Blast radius is the set of resources and effects reachable by faulty or adversarial execution under a stated threat model and enforcement boundary, including what it can read, change, disclose, or consume.

  1. Significance: It connects the task’s intended scope to the concrete filesystem, process, compute, and communication limits the runtime must enforce.
  2. Distinction: An authorization grant defines what a task is permitted to do. Blast radius measures the cumulative resources and state running code could compromise or mutate under failure or attack, including misuse within granted permissions.
  3. Common pitfall: Estimating execution reach from the high-level tools declared to the model, rather than from the operating system privileges available to the code executing behind them.

A threat model must also delineate which classes of failure it covers. In agent systems, these fall into three tiers. The first is ordinary application errors (such as a faulty test suite exhausting memory). The second is hostile code executing inside the guest environment (such as an injected shell script scanning local network interfaces). The last is compromise of the isolation mechanism itself (such as a kernel privilege escalation or container breakout). An envelope engineered to contain accidental test failures may collapse when confronted with an active kernel exploit. The third tier is why every sandbox design must name its trusted computing base: the hardware and software components whose correct operation the isolation depends on, which untrusted code must therefore be unable to modify or subvert. A kernel shared with the guest belongs to it; so does a hypervisor, a policy-enforcing proxy, or the supervisor process that installs the limits. Selecting an execution envelope therefore requires cataloging that base and every physical and operating system resource shared between guest and host.

8.2.2 Blast radius by authority level

How wide a blast radius the sandbox must bound depends on the task’s H·S·A authority level. A read-only (\(\AuthorityExposureLevel_0\)) summarization task needs no file writes to exfiltrate credentials over an open socket. Resetting a local container after a compensable (\(\AuthorityExposureLevel_2\)) or irreversible (\(\AuthorityExposureLevel_3\)) tool call, by contrast, cannot undo a payment already transmitted to an external API. Table 1 maps each authority level to the exposures the runtime governs and the containment obligations they create.

↩︎ The H·S·A authority levels \(\AuthorityExposureLevel_0\) through \(\AuthorityExposureLevel_3\) are established in The H·S·A Exposure Space.

Write protections keep a file intact at \(\AuthorityExposureLevel_0\), but only read isolation and network egress policies prevent confidential data from leaving the host. Conversely, at \(\AuthorityExposureLevel_2\) and \(\AuthorityExposureLevel_3\), actions such as processing a financial payment or updating a remote database land outside the disposable local workspace. Confining the local shell process does nothing to reverse an unintended external transaction once transmitted. Preventing external harms requires gating side effects behind mediation proxies and explicit authorization checks; validating that a command conforms to policy remains distinct from verifying that it aligns with the user’s actual goal.

Containment beneath the model (principle 9) requires the execution boundary to be enforced outside the code being constrained. An isolation mechanism must be implemented by a layer below the code it controls (Saltzer and Kaashoek 2009). The tool layer required its reference monitor to be tamper-proof, always invoked, and small enough to verify. The sandbox must meet the same three requirements for the code those calls start, and the third is why the trusted computing base named in the threat model should stay small. This is invariant closure (principle 1) at the execution boundary. Model-side measures lower the probability of an unsafe proposal, but only a mechanism the running code cannot modify bounds what that code can reach.

Saltzer, Jerome H., and M. Frans Kaashoek. 2009. Principles of Computer System Design: An Introduction. Morgan Kaufmann.

↩︎ The reference monitor and its three requirements (tamper-proof, always invoked, small enough to verify) are established in Complete mediation.

In the repository scenario, the README text steers two requests through the same boundaries (figure 2). The first is the injected run_shell command. The second uses a channel the task legitimately holds, because the same README asks the agent to attach source files to the diagnostic report it is permitted to send through a submit_report tool. The run_shell request is well typed, so tool admission passes it; the filesystem policy then stops it at the read of ~/.aws/credentials. The destination policy would also refuse its upload, but the two checks guard different things. The read check protects the credential file, and the egress check protects the route out of the host. Neither duplicates the other, so had the credential been readable by some other route, the egress check alone would decide the outcome. The submit_report request, carrying private source, passes every gate because each of its steps is allowed, and its disclosure reaches the approved service without any boundary being escaped. Layering boundaries therefore narrows what an attack can do without bounding what a permitted channel can carry, a gap section 8.7.4 returns to. Each boundary must also log its decisions, since the absence of an error report is never evidence of safety.

Execution boundaries are defined by lifetime as well as resource scope. During a multi-turn repair task, edits to src/pool.py and intermediate test artifacts must persist across turns to support iterative debugging, yet that state must never leak into the initial environment of a later tenant or task. Within a turn, test runners need a writable scratch directory while the acceptance test suite itself stays mounted read-only outside the agent’s reach, so the model cannot pass a suite by deleting its assertions. When the task ends, the runtime must stop background child processes, reclaim virtual memory and scratch storage, and reset every state domain covered by the reset contract of section 8.8.1. A cooperative cancellation request neither forces runaway code to yield nor undoes an external network write that has already committed. Execution reach, the trusted computing base, and boundary lifetime therefore form the three core obligations an agent sandbox must discharge, beginning with where untrusted code runs relative to the software enforcing its limits.

8.3 Limits of In-Process Restriction

The repair agent’s compiles and test runs need a process of their own; the temptation to skip one is strongest for small analysis steps. Consider the research agent summarizing a CSV file it retrieved. When the agent generates a Python script to compute summary statistics, an engineer might be tempted to execute that code directly inside the supervisor’s own Python runtime. In-process execution is appealing because it avoids the latency of spawning a child process and the cost of inter-process serialization, and it reuses heavy numerical libraries already resident in memory. However, this convenience co-locates untrusted, model-generated code with the supervisor’s private state, database connections, and operational credentials. Attempting to sanitize this environment by stripping dangerous functions from the script’s global namespace confuses an interface filter with an execution boundary. Although schema and argument checks verify that the agent requested an analysis operation, the arbitrary code carried within that payload executes with the full operational reach of the host process.

8.3.1 The reachable object graph

Suppose a runtime developer attempts to isolate execution by passing an empty __builtins__ dictionary and a single helper function, such as total, to Python’s eval() or exec(). The intended interface allows the script to sum numbers while withholding access to system calls. In ordinary Python, however, user-defined functions retain a live reference to the global namespace of the module where they were defined (func.__globals__). As demonstrated in listing 1, generated code can traverse this reference directly from the permitted helper function, recovering the supervisor’s module scope without ever invoking import or calling globals().

This escape assumes only a standard Python function and unrestricted attribute access. The exposed module namespace often contains imported packages, configuration values, active network clients, and raw system primitives that the supervisor never intended to expose. Once code gains access to these objects, its reach expands to match the full operating system permissions of the supervisor process. The failure occurs long before the program reads a protected file or opens a network socket, because the interface boundary fails at the level of the language runtime itself.

Patching this vulnerability by blocking access to .__globals__ merely invites alternate traversal paths. A denylist that filters specific attribute names must account for every object passed into the execution scope and every reference reachable through their class hierarchies. In dynamic languages, string-based filters fail because attribute names can be assembled at run time using dynamic lookups, such as getattr(obj, "__" + "globals" + "__"). Any literal value also provides a path to the universal base class object, from which an attacker can enumerate all subclasses loaded into memory, including classes that manipulate files and spawn processes.1

1 Subclass Walk: The expression ().__class__.__base__.__subclasses__() goes from an empty tuple to object and returns the classes derived directly from it, from which every loaded class can be reached by recursion, including classes that wrap files and processes. It uses no builtin name and no import, so a filter that deletes open and __import__ leaves the process’s reach unchanged.

Rice, Henry Gordon. 1953. “Classes of Recursively Enumerable Sets and Their Decision Problems.” Transactions of the American Mathematical Society 74 (2): 358–66. https://doi.org/10.1090/S0002-9947-1953-0053041-6.

The shown escape follows from an accessible object reference inside the shared interpreter, not from undecidability. Rice’s theorem (Rice 1953) addresses a broader limit: no classifier can decide every non-trivial semantic property of unrestricted Turing-complete programs. A classifier that admits all programs that never invoke a process-launching object and rejects every program that eventually does cannot be both sound and complete. A conservative filter may instead reject legitimate programs, and a restricted language may make its permitted behavior decidable. Effective confinement of arbitrary code must govern what execution can reach through its environment, rather than rely on a perfect classification of its source text.

Restricted languages and domain-specific evaluators remain useful in systems design. A purpose-built arithmetic evaluator can safely process formulas over supplied variables, and an isolated bytecode engine can enforce strict constraints on memory allocation and host imports. Such designs succeed because they enforce explicit capability models with formal resource guarantees, and the conditions are specific enough to check. In-process evaluation is safe only when all of the following hold. The language’s operands must be the values the supervisor supplied, so no reference leads back to the supervisor’s objects. The language must have no attribute access, calls, imports, or unbounded loops, so the reachable set cannot grow during evaluation. Its worst-case cost must be bounded by the size of the input, so a hostile expression cannot stall the supervisor that must stay responsive to every other task. A guard such as p99_ms < 400 and error_rate < 0.01, which the remediation agent attaches to a proposed configuration change and the supervisor evaluates over supplied metrics, meets all three. A single regular-expression operator with a backtracking matcher breaks the third, because its running time can grow exponentially in the input length. Designs that meet the conditions must not be conflated with running arbitrary Python code inside an interpreter whose globals have merely been pruned.

8.3.2 Native libraries and the failure domain

Even if an interpreter blocked every Python-level reference escape, data analysis scripts routinely invoke native extensions in C, C++, or Fortran to manipulate multidimensional arrays, parse structured files, and run numerical kernels. When those extensions execute in-process, their memory accesses take place directly in the supervisor’s address space, bypassing interpreter-level restrictions entirely. An out-of-bounds write or use-after-free in a C or C++ parser running inside the supervisor process can overwrite the supervisor’s heap, expose its in-memory API keys, or execute arbitrary machine instructions with the supervisor’s privileges.

A native-code compromise collapses the boundary at the operating system level. System calls issued from a compromised thread pass the kernel’s access checks because the kernel identifies the thread as the supervisor process. The compromised thread can then expose the supervisor’s credentials, terminate concurrent tasks, and halt enforcement of the broader tool contract. The underlying design question is which components belong to the trusted computing base. In-process evaluation places the language interpreter, user-defined helpers, third-party native libraries, and the supervisor’s orchestration logic into a single failure domain. Stripping symbol names from a script leaves all of these shared components exposed to memory corruption.

8.3.3 Shared authority and shared resources

Spawning a separate child process isolates virtual memory, but a child process launched under the developer’s OS user account still inherits open file descriptors, active database sockets, environment-variable tokens, and full read-write access to the developer’s home directory. Isolating address spaces does not automatically restrict ambient authority, the reach a process holds by virtue of the identity it runs as rather than because the task handed it anything. Zero ambient authority, which the introduction requires of the model, must therefore also hold for the processes its tool calls start. Inherited file handles maintain access even when the worker possesses no code to open those files by path, so sandboxing requires explicit control over which identities, descriptors, sockets, and filesystem mounts a worker process receives.

↩︎ Zero ambient authority, the requirement that every side effect pass through an explicit grant, is established in The fail-plausible fault.

System availability likewise demands an enforced boundary. A script can consume CPU cycles indefinitely, exhaust memory allocations, or flood the local disk with temporary files without ever reading a secret or transmitting a network packet. Permitted operations such as file creation or child process spawning can exhaust system-wide file descriptor tables or process table capacity. Namespace filtering cannot budget or bound these resources. If execution occurs within the supervisor process, halting a runaway loop or reclaiming leaked memory requires terminating the supervisor itself.

An execution boundary must therefore budget and enforce task-scoped limits from outside the worker: CPU cores, wall-clock time, hard memory ceilings, process counts, storage footprint, and standard output volume. When execution stalls or exceeds its quota, the supervisor steps in to throttle the group, reject further requests, or terminate the worker process, stopping further compute even though termination cannot undo external side effects that have already committed. Section 8.5 compares the isolation envelopes that modern operating systems and hypervisors provide to enforce these limits. The runtime must first decide, however, which authorities may cross the boundary at all, because a child process that inherits the supervisor’s credentials has its own address space and is still unconfined.

8.4 Capabilities and Credentials

In the README attack, the task needs repository access, not the developer’s credentials, and the runtime must carry that distinction into the resources the executing code can reach. A run_shell worker launched with the developer’s identity is the confused deputy of definition 7.3 moved one layer down. The GitHub MCP leak showed a tool adapter spending the user’s repository authority on an attacker’s behalf, and here a subprocess spends the developer’s file and network authority on behalf of README text. Identity-based access control can express narrow permissions through an access control list;2 the failure is letting the request exercise the service’s broader authority instead of the authority delegated for this task. That failure comes naturally under ambient authority, since an ordinary process exercises whatever its identity can reach. Capabilities, introduced by Dennis and Van Horn (1966) for multiprogrammed systems, make the delegation explicit instead (Saltzer and Schroeder 1975; Miller 2006).

2 Access Matrix: Lampson (1974) described protection as a matrix of subjects by objects whose entries list the permitted operations. An access control list stores one column with its object; a capability list stores one row with its subject. The choice decides whether a request is checked against who is asking or against what the requester was handed, and only the second can express “this task, this file, this operation” without a new identity per task.

Lampson, Butler W. 1974. “Protection.” ACM Operating Systems Review 8 (1): 18–24. https://doi.org/10.1145/775265.775268.
Miller, Mark Samuel. 2006. “Robust Composition: Towards a Unified Approach to Access Control and Concurrency Control.” PhD thesis, Johns Hopkins University.

↩︎ The confused deputy, and the GitHub MCP leak that illustrates it, are established in Discovery and authority.

Definition 8.2: Capability

A capability is an unforgeable reference that both designates a resource and authorizes specific operations on it, so that holding the reference is the permission to use it.

  1. Significance: It lets the runtime hand executing code exactly the objects and operations of the task grant, bounding the code’s blast radius by what it was given rather than by what its identity can reach.
  2. Distinction: Under ambient authority a process can reach everything its identity may touch, named globally; under capabilities the default reach is empty, and every access goes through a reference the holder received.
  3. Common pitfall: Treating a plain identifier, or a token the model can read and rewrite, as a capability. Without a protected binding the holder can forge or substitute a broader reference.

The goal both models serve is least privilege, the rule that each process holds only the authority its current task needs, so that a fault or an injected instruction can misuse no more than that task’s grant. Ambient authority grants more than the task needs by default, and capabilities make the rule enforceable. The two models differ in where authority comes from, how resources are named, and how authority is passed on and revoked (table 2). For the README attack the decisive row is default reach. Under ambient authority the injected command starts with everything the developer’s identity can touch, including ~/.aws/credentials. Under capabilities it starts with nothing and holds only the references the task grant handed it.

↩︎ Least privilege applied to the separate operations on a job handle is established in Job handles.

8.4.1 Scoped grants

Turning capability-based authority into an enforceable runtime check requires an unforgeable representation. If a runtime represents a task grant as a plain file path or an unsigned string that the worker process can inspect and edit, the worker can substitute ~/.aws/credentials or rewrite "read" to "write" to widen its own reach. A capability must instead be a protected handle checked by a runtime or kernel, or a cryptographic token verified by a service; it need not be a string visible to the model at all. Either way, the enforcement layer prevents the holder from fabricating broader rights or substituting another task’s reference.

Use \(\CapabilityGrant = (\GrantRights, \mathcal{O}_\CapabilityGrant, \GrantCondition, \GrantExpiryTime)\) as an abstract description of a grant: rights \(\GrantRights\), objects \(\mathcal{O}_\CapabilityGrant\), condition predicate \(\GrantCondition\), and expiry time \(\GrantExpiryTime\). The tuple describes authority independent of any token encoding. Its conditions can pair different operations with different objects and bind use to a task or recipient, so read and write listed among the rights need not both apply to every listed object.

The tuple becomes operational as a check. A proposed access \(\ProposedAccess\) names an operation, a target, and its arguments. The enforcement point admits it at time \(\AccessAttemptTime\) only if every condition in equation 1 holds:

\[ \text{admit}(\ProposedAccess, \CapabilityGrant, \AccessAttemptTime) \iff \text{authentic}(\CapabilityGrant) \land \ProposedAccess.\text{op} \in \GrantRights \land \ProposedAccess.\text{target} \sqsubseteq \mathcal{O}_\CapabilityGrant \land \GrantCondition(\ProposedAccess) \land \AccessAttemptTime < \GrantExpiryTime \tag{1}\]

Here \(\text{authentic}(\CapabilityGrant)\) means the reference is protected and bound to this holder, whether by a kernel handle table or by a message authentication code the issuer can verify. The relation \(\ProposedAccess.\text{target} \sqsubseteq \mathcal{O}_\CapabilityGrant\) means the resolved target lies within the designated objects for that operation. A rejected access changes nothing and returns a structured observation the model can act on. The check is also cheap relative to what it guards. Verifying a handle or a message authentication code is a local computation, orders of magnitude shorter than the model call that produced the proposal, so mediating every access costs nothing a user would notice. The expensive part of capability enforcement is making sure no path bypasses the check.

For the repair, table 3 separates the resources an execution grant could cover. Paths are names relative to the task’s designated workspace, not arbitrary host paths.

The grant also has a lifetime and enforced limits on execution and output. It permits the repair to make progress while separating writable candidate artifacts from the machinery used to assess them. Tests keep their writable temporary files; protecting the acceptance checks means protecting their implementation and reported results.

Binding that grant to execution requires resolving the target inside the task’s scope and checking the required right and conditions before any effect takes place, using resource names that cannot be redirected to a different object by symlinks or concurrent renames. Because authority to edit src/pool.py says nothing about whether the file’s current contents match the model’s assumptions, the version precondition of Conditional updates remains a separate check at the moment of the write. The executing code must then be unable to bypass the policy by opening another path, using an inherited handle, or contacting an unrestricted service directly, which can require restricted mounts, process identities, mediated interfaces, or other controls supplied by the envelope. Once the enforcement layer has bound the guest’s accessible resources to the grant, the guest need not attach a token to every system call; what matters is the resulting reach.

On Linux, the supervisor can act as a policy compiler. It verifies the grant, then translates the permitted paths and operations into kernel-enforced rules before it starts the worker. Landlock (The Linux Kernel Documentation Project 2024b), a Linux security module, lets an unprivileged process irreversibly restrict the filesystem hierarchies and network ports it and its descendants can access. A seccomp-bpf filter restricts which system calls the process may make and with which scalar arguments. The supervisor installs both, together with restricted mounts and an unprivileged identity, and then calls execve on the tool. The worker runs under limits the kernel enforces on every open and connect, without knowing the grant’s format and without being able to remove the rules it inherited. Filtering on system-call numbers cannot inspect the contents of pointer arguments safely,3 which is why path restrictions belong to a mechanism that resolves paths inside the kernel rather than to the system-call filter.

The Linux Kernel Documentation Project. 2024b. Landlock: Unprivileged Access Control. Linux kernel documentation.

3 Check-Then-Use Races: Early user-level sandboxes confined untrusted helper programs by intercepting each system call and checking its arguments (Goldberg et al. 1996). Garfinkel (2003) catalogued why that design is fragile: a path argument lives in memory the confined process controls, so another thread can change it after the check and before the kernel reads it, and a filter that imperfectly replicates kernel semantics misses indirect routes to the same resource. Checking the resolved object inside the kernel at the moment of use closes that window, which is what an in-kernel access-control module such as Landlock does.

Goldberg, Ian, David Wagner, Randi Thomas, and Eric A. Brewer. 1996. “A Secure Environment for Untrusted Helper Applications: Confining the Wily Hacker.” Proceedings of the 6th USENIX Security Symposium.
Garfinkel, Tal. 2003. “Traps and Pitfalls: Practical Problems in System Call Interposition Based Security Tools.” Proceedings of the Network and Distributed System Security Symposium (NDSS).

8.4.2 Narrowing delegated authority

When a repair trajectory delegates a subtask to a helper process or transitions from editing src/pool.py to running the test suite, handing over the full parent grant lets the helper modify files outside its subtask. Minting an unchecked child grant, by contrast, risks accidentally adding execute or write rights the parent never held. Attenuation prevents privilege escalation during delegation by deriving a child grant that permits no more than its parent. Writing \(\GrantAllowedAccesses(\CapabilityGrant)\) for the accesses allowed by a grant, the relation requires

\[ \ChildCapabilityGrant\sqsubseteq \ParentCapabilityGrant \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \GrantAllowedAccesses(\ChildCapabilityGrant)\subseteq\GrantAllowedAccesses(\ParentCapabilityGrant). \]

Narrower object scope, fewer rights, stronger conditions, and an earlier expiry can establish that relation. Consider the repair’s edit grant, issued at time \(\GrantIssueTime_0\), and two proposed children. All three carry the same condition \(\GrantCondition\), which binds use to this task, so the tuples below list only the components that differ:

\[ \begin{aligned} \EditPhaseCapabilityGrant &= \big(\GrantRights=\{\text{read}, \text{write}\},\ \mathcal{O}_\CapabilityGrant=\texttt{repo/},\ \GrantExpiryTime=\GrantIssueTime_0 + 300\,\text{s}\big) \\ \TestPhaseCapabilityGrant &= \big(\GrantRights=\{\text{read}, \text{execute}\},\ \mathcal{O}_\CapabilityGrant=\texttt{repo/},\ \GrantExpiryTime=\GrantIssueTime_0 + 120\,\text{s}\big) && \text{rejected: execute} \notin \EditGrantRights \\ \ScanCapabilityGrant &= \big(\GrantRights=\{\text{read}\},\ \mathcal{O}_\CapabilityGrant=\texttt{repo/src/pool.py},\ \GrantExpiryTime=\GrantIssueTime_0 + 120\,\text{s}\big) && \text{valid: } \ScanCapabilityGrant \sqsubseteq \EditPhaseCapabilityGrant \end{aligned} \]

The test grant fails even though its lifetime is shorter, because it asks for a right the parent never held. Execution needs an appropriate parent grant or a separately authorized grant from the controller. A runtime that intends to support a test phase therefore includes execute in the grant it issues at the start, or issues a new grant for that phase. It then actively withdraws write authority over the source tree when testing begins, which freezes the candidate being graded. Task-local scratch is a separate object in the grant and stays writable, so the tests keep their temporary files. The read-only mount of the acceptance checks is a second layer beneath the grant. Even a grant such as \(\EditPhaseCapabilityGrant\) that names all of repo/ cannot write through that mount. Together the two layers keep a candidate from modifying the fixtures and checks that grade it, the property sealed evaluation depends on.

↪ Sealed evaluation criteria held outside the agent’s reach are developed in Grader isolation.

Birgisson, Arnór, Joseph G. Politz, Úlfar Erlingsson, Ankur Taly, Michael Vrable, and Mark S. Miller. 2014. “Macaroons: Cookies with Contextual Caveats for Decentralized Authorization in the Cloud.” Proceedings of the Network and Distributed System Security Symposium (NDSS).

Attenuation can also happen without contacting the issuer. Chained-caveat tokens such as macaroons (Birgisson et al. 2014) let any holder append a restriction and pass on the narrower token. The issuer mints a root token with a message authentication code \(\TokenAuthenticationCode_0\) computed under a secret key only it holds. A holder adds caveat \(\TokenCaveat_i\) (a path prefix, a rights subset, a turn limit, an earlier expiry) and replaces the code with a keyed hash (HMAC) of the previous code and the caveat,

\[ \TokenAuthenticationCode_i = \text{HMAC}(\TokenAuthenticationCode_{i-1},\, \TokenCaveat_i). \tag{2}\]

The verifier recomputes the chain of equation 2 from its root key and admits a request only if every caveat is satisfied. A holder of the child cannot remove a caveat without the prior code \(\TokenAuthenticationCode_{i-1}\), which the chain does not reveal, so a subagent or tool worker can narrow authority offline but cannot widen the token it received.

This relation constrains derived authority; a trajectory can still move from inspection to an authorized edit or test phase under explicit policy. Deriving a read-only child also leaves a broader parent that the same holder retained fully usable. If a phase transition is meant to remove write authority, the runtime withdraws or disables the earlier grant and deals with access already opened under it. Delegation must also preserve aggregate budgets: several children together receive at most the parent’s resource allowance. Both forms of attenuation share this limit (figure 3). A checked derivation narrows what a child may do but leaves the parent usable, and a chained-caveat token narrows what its next holder may do but cannot reach back to restrict a copy of the token someone already holds.

The operative test is therefore which grants and resource handles the worker can still exercise, rather than merely whether the child grant is narrower. This test applies equally to a helper process inside one trajectory and to a subagent that receives a delegated grant, since passing a grant to another agent leaves the meaning of authority unchanged.

8.4.3 Expiry and revocation

Suppose the repair task’s 300-second edit phase expires while a worker process still holds an open writable file descriptor to src/pool.py. Rejecting new open() calls after \(\GrantExpiryTime\) stops fresh handles from being created, but the already-open descriptor continues to permit writes unless the runtime closes the handle or stops the worker. An expiry timestamp states when a grant stops authorizing new use; revoking active access requires an enforcement mechanism that checks status on each operation, closes live handles, and terminates or constrains ongoing processes. When the edit phase ends, the transition must therefore stop or constrain the worker, revoke the usable reference where supported, or move subsequent work into a restricted execution context. The runtime must also record what it has actually confirmed rather than equating a changed policy record with completed revocation.

External effects have their own boundary. A broker can stop accepting new requests after expiry, but a previously admitted remote operation may still complete. A lease or cancellation request does not settle that effect or justify a retry. Environment teardown and readiness for reuse receive their full treatment in section 8.8.1; here the requirement is that authority have an enforced lifetime rather than an advisory timestamp.

↩︎ Why an unsettled remote outcome does not justify a blind retry is established in Retrying Uncertain Actions.

Checkpoint 8.1: Scope, attenuation, and lifetime

A repair worker holds read and write access to the candidate source. It derives a read-only grant for src/pool.py, retains the original grant, and opens a writable handle before the edit phase expires.

8.4.4 Credential brokering

Scoped file and process grants still leave a placement decision for external service secrets. Placing a private repository token inside the guest’s environment variables or .netrc file so the repair worker can run git fetch exposes that raw secret to any injected shell command or environment dump that reads process memory or disk. A credential broker instead holds the secret outside the guest and uses it on behalf of an authorized request. For example, when the repair worker requests a fetch from its approved private repository through a host-owned source-service adapter, the broker checks the bound task identity, repository, operation, destination, and current grant, then performs the credentialed request without returning the credential. Permission to fetch does not permit a push or access to another repository, so the worker can cause a narrowly authorized use of the credential without possessing the credential itself.

Brokering requires a supported mediation arrangement. An application adapter can make the upstream request directly; a proxy can attach credentials only where it can legitimately inspect and modify the relevant protocol exchange. A signing service likewise needs to authorize what it signs, not merely conceal its key. Section 8.7.1 develops how the runtime constrains routes that might bypass these interfaces. If a tool genuinely requires a credential inside the guest, a narrowly scoped, short-lived credential shrinks the scope and window of exposure, while the guest still possesses it for that window.

↪ Tracking whether an allowed request’s data may flow to its recipient is developed in Information-Flow Policy.

Keeping selected credentials outside the guest removes the most valuable target from the README attack, and from the environment dump in the Gemini CLI incident. The environment and repository content remain sensitive, and a brokered operation can still be misused within its permitted scope. Secret placement, access permission, and whether an allowed request’s data may flow to its recipient are three distinct concerns. A sandbox governs the first two, where authority resides and how execution is confined to it. The third is an information-flow problem that a sandbox alone cannot settle. Capabilities and brokering specify the resources the runtime intends to expose, and enforcing those limits in practice requires an execution envelope evaluated by what it shares with the host and which components uphold its boundary.

8.5 Choosing an Isolation Envelope

A lightweight log parser, a multi-turn software repair task compiling code and executing test suites, and an untrusted customer-uploaded binary demand different operating interfaces. Selecting an appropriate execution envelope begins with these workload requirements and the host resources that must remain protected, rather than defaulting to a convenient packaging format.

Definition 8.3: Isolation envelope

An isolation envelope is the enforced boundary around executing code, specified by the resources it exposes, the components it shares with the host or other workloads, and the mechanisms that restrict access and consumption.

  1. Significance: It makes the permitted reach of an operation a property of its execution environment rather than a request to the model or program.
  2. Distinction: A grant specifies permitted authority, whereas the envelope enforces that boundary against executing code. Enforcing confinement guarantees that code cannot exceed its assigned reach, but it does not validate whether every permitted operation aligns with the agent’s intended task.
  3. Common pitfall: Treating the high-level label of a container, virtual machine, or language runtime as a complete isolation policy without auditing its concrete configuration, host mounts, and exposed system interfaces.

Choosing a different envelope changes both the composition of the trusted computing base defined in the threat model and the interface untrusted code can reach. Narrowing that interface simplifies security review, but counting system calls or source lines cannot produce a quantitative probability of sandbox escape. A systems comparison instead asks which failure modes each envelope contains, which components remain trusted, and which operating interfaces the workload requires. The four envelopes in figure 4 differ first in where a system call lands: in the host kernel after a filter, in an emulating user-space kernel, in a separate guest kernel, or nowhere at all, because restricted bytecode has no system-call instruction.

8.5.1 Shared and interposed kernels

The first two columns of figure 4 start from a standard POSIX process and differ in whether its system calls reach the host kernel directly. When untrusted code issues a permitted system call inside an ordinary container, the host kernel services that call itself, so a single exploitable bug in the shared kernel compromises the entire host. A shared-kernel container confines host processes using native operating-system primitives. Linux namespaces restrict views of global resources such as process trees, network interfaces, and mount tables. Access controls and seccomp filters restrict permissible operations. Control groups bound resource consumption. Container isolation is therefore only as strong as the host kernel and the configuration around it; packaging an application into an image provides no security boundary on its own.

To avoid exposing the host kernel directly, a user-space kernel places an intermediary kernel implementation between the application and the host operating system. For example, gVisor’s Sentry reimplements the Linux kernel API as a memory-safe user-space process written in Go (Google 2019). The Sentry intercepts application system calls, services them internally without passing the raw call through to the host kernel, and requests host resources through a restricted seccomp filter. An envelope must still be audited by its concrete configuration rather than its name, because the trusted path can change under an unchanged label. The original gVisor design routed every filesystem operation through a separate proxy process so that the Sentry held no host file descriptors, whereas a later revision lets the Sentry open paths itself relative to descriptors handed to it at startup (Ranjan 2023). Because compilers and build tools depend on specific POSIX behaviors, an engineering team must also verify that the user-space kernel supports the system calls its toolchain needs, and pair the emulator with independent storage and compute quotas.

Google. 2019. gVisor: Container Runtime Sandbox.
Ranjan, Ayush. 2023. Faster Filesystem Access with Directfs. gVisor project blog.

8.5.2 Separate guest kernels

When a build or repair task needs full Linux system-call compatibility without exposing the host kernel to guest exploits, user-space emulation can fall short on either coverage or speed. A microVM instead runs a real guest kernel and its workload behind a hardware-virtualization boundary managed by a virtual machine monitor (VMM). Application system calls execute natively inside the guest kernel, and only interactions with the host cross explicitly configured virtualization and device interfaces. Compromising the guest kernel therefore does not hand control of the host to the attacker; security rests on the monitor, the hypervisor, the CPU virtualization extensions, and the virtual devices exposed to the guest.

Hardware-assisted virtualization roots this isolation directly in processor architectural state (Uhlig et al. 2005). The CPU runs the hypervisor in VMX root operation and executes the guest, kernel and applications alike, in VMX non-root operation. Ordinary instructions in non-root operation execute natively, including the guest’s own system-call instructions (syscall or sysenter), which trap into the guest kernel without hypervisor intervention. Sensitive operations,4 such as modifying control registers, reprogramming interrupt controllers, or accessing unmapped physical device registers, trigger a VM exit. During a VM exit, the CPU hardware automatically saves guest register state into the hypervisor-managed Virtual Machine Control Structure (VMCS) and transfers execution control to the hypervisor. Memory translation proceeds through two distinct stages: the guest page tables translate guest virtual addresses to guest physical addresses, and hypervisor-controlled Extended Page Tables (EPT) or Nested Page Tables (NPT)5 translate guest physical addresses to host physical memory:

4 Sensitive Instructions: Popek and Goldberg (1974) stated the classical condition for an efficient virtual machine monitor: every instruction that reads or changes machine control state must trap when run unprivileged, so that the monitor sees it. The x86 architecture had such instructions that did not trap, and hardware extensions added a separate guest mode in which those operations exit to the hypervisor (Uhlig et al. 2005). That exit is the enforcement point: guest code cannot change host control state without passing through code the host trusts.

Popek, Gerald J., and Robert P. Goldberg. 1974. “Formal Requirements for Virtualizable Third Generation Architectures.” Communications of the ACM 17 (7): 412–21. https://doi.org/10.1145/361011.361073.
Uhlig, Rich, Gil Neiger, Dion Rodgers, Amy L. Santoni, Fernando C. M. Martins, Andrew V. Anderson, Steven M. Bennett, Alain Kagi, Felix H. Leung, and Larry Smith. 2005. “Intel Virtualization Technology.” Computer 38 (5): 48–56. https://doi.org/10.1109/MC.2005.163.

5 Two-Dimensional Page Walk: On a TLB miss the hardware must translate each guest page-table pointer through the hypervisor’s tables as well, so a four-level walk in each dimension can need up to twenty-four memory references instead of four (Bhargava et al. 2008). Page-walk caches reduce that cost, but a memory-intensive test suite inside a microVM pays more per TLB miss than the same suite in a container. The second mapping that isolates the guest is also a per-miss tax on its memory accesses.

Bhargava, Ravi, Benjamin Serebrin, Francesco Spadini, and Srilatha Manne. 2008. “Accelerating Two-Dimensional Page Walks for Virtualized Systems.” Proceedings of the 13th International Conference on Architectural Support for Programming Languages and Operating Systems (ASPLOS), 26–35. https://doi.org/10.1145/1346281.1346286.

\[ \text{guest virtual} \xrightarrow{\text{guest page tables}} \text{guest physical} \xrightarrow{\text{hypervisor page tables}} \text{host physical} \]

Even if an untrusted agent exploits a kernel vulnerability inside the guest and gains root privileges, it can rewrite only the first mapping. Because the guest kernel cannot modify the hypervisor’s second-stage page tables, it remains physically incapable of addressing host memory frames that the hypervisor has not explicitly mapped into its address space. This hardware boundary is what separates a guest kernel compromise from a host compromise.

Firecracker exemplifies this minimalist approach, building on the Linux Kernel-based Virtual Machine (KVM) subsystem (Kivity et al. 2007). To minimize both cold-start latency and reachable attack surface, Firecracker originally stripped away legacy PC hardware. It omits a BIOS, dispenses with PCI bus emulation, provides minimal virtio-net and virtio-block devices alongside a basic serial console and minimal keyboard controller, and boots an uncompressed guest kernel directly (Agache et al. 2020). Subsequent releases introduced ACPI support and an optional virtio-pci transport (Firecracker Project 2025), reiterating that the exposed attack surface depends entirely on the specific version and runtime flags deployed. Furthermore, Firecracker establishes a clean architectural separation: while the VMM exposes virtual network interfaces to the guest, host-side networking infrastructure (such as iptables, nftables, or eBPF programs) enforces outbound egress filtering. The agent’s external network policy is thus governed by the host orchestration plane rather than entrusted to the guest (Firecracker Project 2024).

Firecracker Project. 2025. Firecracker Changelog. Firecracker source repository.
Firecracker Project. 2024. Firecracker Design. Firecracker documentation.

8.5.3 Restricted bytecode and host imports

Not every tool call needs a guest kernel or a POSIX process: a structured log parser that runs for a few milliseconds per call cannot afford even a fast virtual-machine restore on every invocation. A bytecode sandbox6 instead executes code compiled to an intermediate representation whose runtime governs memory accesses and control flow directly. WebAssembly, for example, confines guest code to a contiguous, linear memory space that the runtime isolates using software bounds checks or hardware page-table protections with unmapped guard pages (Haas et al. 2017). WebAssembly defines no native system-call instruction; guest modules interact with the outside world only by calling host functions explicitly imported at instantiation. Those memory boundaries protect the host from the module rather than separating data structures inside the module from one another, and any imported host function runs with host privileges to perform whatever action its implementation allows.

6 Software Fault Isolation: Wahbe et al. (1993) showed that an untrusted module can be confined to its own region of a shared address space by rewriting its code so that every store and indirect jump is forced into that region, with no hardware protection-domain switch. Bounds-checked linear memory in WebAssembly applies the same confinement to a portable format (Haas et al. 2017). The consequence is the cost row of table 4: a call into the host is a function call rather than a trap, which is why this envelope suits short per-call tools.

Wahbe, Robert, Steven Lucco, Thomas E. Anderson, and Susan L. Graham. 1993. “Efficient Software-Based Fault Isolation.” Proceedings of the Fourteenth ACM Symposium on Operating Systems Principles (SOSP), 203–16. https://doi.org/10.1145/168619.168635.
Haas, Andreas, Andreas Rossberg, Derek L. Schuff, Ben L. Titzer, Michael Holman, Dan Gohman, Luke Wagner, Alon Zakai, and JF Bastien. 2017. “Bringing the Web up to Speed with WebAssembly.” Proceedings of the 38th ACM SIGPLAN Conference on Programming Language Design and Implementation (PLDI), 185–200. https://doi.org/10.1145/3062341.3062363.

7 Capability Mode: Capsicum brought the same discipline to UNIX processes (Watson et al. 2010). A process in capability mode loses access to global namespaces such as absolute paths and can open files only relative to directory descriptors it already holds, so the descriptors its parent passed in are the whole of its filesystem reach. Preopened handles in a bytecode sandbox and a supervisor’s grant installed before execve are both ways of handing over the capability before untrusted code starts.

Watson, Robert N. M., Jonathan Anderson, Ben Laurie, and Kris Kennaway. 2010. “Capsicum: Practical Capabilities for UNIX.” Proceedings of the 19th USENIX Security Symposium.

The WebAssembly System Interface (WASI) maps directly to the capability-based security model introduced in section 8.4. During module initialization, the host pre-opens specific directory file descriptors and binds them to the sandbox environment. Every subsequent guest path lookup must resolve relative to one of these pre-opened descriptors;7 any directory traversal attempting to escape the assigned root (such as ../../etc/passwd) immediately fails with a capability-violation error before reaching host filesystem primitives.

Bytecode sandboxing fits workloads that compile to WebAssembly and need only narrow host interactions, such as a log parser that reads an input buffer and returns a sanitized record without touching the filesystem or network sockets. Supplying broad host imports, however, re-opens the attack surface, because the runtime, its ahead-of-time or just-in-time compiler, and every imported host function belong to the trusted computing base. Execution time and linear memory growth must also be capped so a module cannot stall or exhaust the host.

8.5.4 The system-call tax

A dependency install, Git checkout, compilation, or test run issues tens of thousands to millions of system calls to read timers, open files, map memory, and spawn subprocesses, so even a few microseconds of mediation overhead per call can add seconds to a build. Because the four envelopes handle operating-system requests at different boundaries, each pays a different combination of up to three per-call costs, which together make up the envelope’s system-call tax:

\[ \SystemCallOverheadTime = \PrivilegeTrapTime + \SystemCallFilterTime + \ExecutionSwitchTime \tag{3}\]

Here \(\PrivilegeTrapTime\) is the hardware cost of crossing a privilege boundary, \(\SystemCallFilterTime\) is the software cost of validating or emulating the requested operation, and \(\ExecutionSwitchTime\) is the context-switch cost of transferring execution control to another thread or process. Table 4 shows which cost terms each envelope pays.

For coding workflows, the sharpest performance split is between user-space kernel emulation and microVM virtualization. A user-space kernel intercepts and emulates every system call, switching context between application and emulator threads, whereas a microVM handles ordinary system calls natively inside its guest kernel and exits to the host monitor only on physical or virtual device I/O. Measurements of user-space kernels confirm that frequent system calls, especially filesystem operations routed through external proxy daemons, pay heavy latency penalties compared to native Linux execution (Young et al. 2019). Illustrative per-call costs show the scale of the gap. Assume a 0.1 μs trap, 0.05 μs of in-kernel filtering, 1.5 μs of emulation, and a 2 μs switch to the emulator. Every 1,000,000 ordinary calls then add about 0.15 s in a container, 0.1 s in a microVM, and 3.6 s in a user-space kernel, 36× the microVM’s cost, before counting the microVM’s device exits. At those rates the tax barely registers when a task issues tens of thousands of calls, but adds seconds to a build that issues millions. Workloads dominated by dense system calls therefore favor microVMs or shared-kernel containers over user-space kernels, whereas pure in-memory computation in a bytecode sandbox avoids operating-system calls altogether. Because exact timings vary with CPU microarchitecture, host kernel settings, and runtime implementation, the per-call terms show where latency comes from rather than fixing universal constants.

Young, Ethan G., Pengfei Zhu, Tyler Caraza-Harter, Andrea C. Arpaci-Dusseau, and Remzi H. Arpaci-Dusseau. 2019. “The True Cost of Containing: A gVisor Case Study.” 11th USENIX Workshop on Hot Topics in Cloud Computing (HotCloud 19).

8.5.5 Envelope selection

No single envelope fits every agent action. Starting a fresh microVM for a few milliseconds of log parsing wastes more time on setup than on work, while running an unreviewed native build inside a shared-kernel container exposes the host kernel to whatever exploit the build triggers. Table 5 compares the four envelopes by trusted components, order-of-magnitude start cost, and typical workload fit, subject to the threat model and the three selection criteria below.

Selecting an envelope depends on three characteristics of the agent’s action: the code it runs, the authority it holds, and how long it holds the envelope. Weighing those three dimensions together determines which isolation boundary in table 5 fits the call and whether its startup cost should be paid per call or amortized across the trajectory:

  1. Workload profile: Pure computation over deterministic inputs maps directly to restricted bytecode. Arbitrary POSIX binaries, package managers, and shell scripts require the broader interface of a user-space kernel or a dedicated microVM. A shared-kernel container remains eligible when its configured boundary and trusted host kernel satisfy the declared threat model; it does not contain compromise of that shared kernel.
  2. Authority level: At \(\AuthorityExposureLevel_1\), where mutation is confined to a discardable sandbox, tool admission checks only the request, so once code runs the envelope must enforce the boundary against the assumed attacker. The repair scenario assumes arbitrary native code, including a compromised guest kernel, must not control host enforcement; this warrants the separate guest kernel of a microVM under that threat model. At \(\AuthorityExposureLevel_2\) and \(\AuthorityExposureLevel_3\), where actions reach external infrastructure, the envelope must be coupled with credential brokers and approval gates, because local containment cannot stop an authorized external call from altering remote state.
  3. Execution horizon: The time over which an envelope is retained decides how its setup is amortized. A setup of 15 ms is prohibitive for a 3 ms parser invoked hundreds of times, but negligible across a multi-turn repair trajectory that runs for minutes (Notebook 8.1).

Applying these criteria to the three workloads that opened the section gives three different envelopes:

  • The structured log parser executes in a lightweight bytecode sandbox created on demand per invocation.
  • The connection-pool repair agent retains a dedicated microVM throughout its entire multi-turn trajectory. A user-space kernel is an alternative under a narrower threat model that keeps the emulator trusted, provided its interfaces and performance support the toolchain.
  • The customer-uploaded binary runs in an ephemeral microVM stripped of all credentials with outbound egress unconditionally denied. A binary that needs more than this profile allows is refused rather than granted relaxed host privileges.

In every case, the security guarantee is defined by the concrete mounts, virtual devices, packet filters, and resource ceilings applied to that envelope. Performance evaluations must measure the complete lifecycle: admission latency, time to first useful work, steady-state throughput, and final teardown overhead under realistic concurrency. A runtime that advertises instant container creation often defers storage setup or package initialization to the first execution turn. Furthermore, startup costs interact directly with envelope lifecycle policies: allocating a fresh envelope for every atomic step trades initialization latency for clean-slate isolation, whereas retaining an envelope across turns amortizes startup at the cost of managing accumulated state.

Setup occupies a smaller share of a fresh call as useful work increases.

Napkin Math 8.1: Paying setup once or repeatedly
Problem: A hypothetical trajectory performs 100 sequential parser calls, each requiring 3 ms of useful execution, and preparing the envelope through readiness for useful work costs 15 ms in the chapter’s illustrative sandbox profile. How much elapsed time does a fresh envelope per call add over one envelope retained for the trajectory, and at what per-call work does the fresh envelope become affordable?

Math: For \(\EnvelopeCallCount\) calls, setup time \(\EnvelopeSetupTime\), and useful work \(\CallUsefulWorkTime\) per call, fresh-per-call execution takes \(\EnvelopeCallCount(\EnvelopeSetupTime+\CallUsefulWorkTime)\) and trajectory reuse takes \(\EnvelopeSetupTime+\EnvelopeCallCount \CallUsefulWorkTime\). Both perform 300 ms of useful work. Fresh-per-call execution adds 1500 ms of setup, totaling 1800 ms; reuse adds one 15 ms setup, totaling 315 ms, a 5.7× difference. Setup is \(\EnvelopeSetupTime/(\EnvelopeSetupTime+\CallUsefulWorkTime)\) = 83.3 percent of every fresh call. Holding setup to at most 10 percent of a fresh call requires \(\CallUsefulWorkTime \ge \EnvelopeSetupTime(1-\SetupShareCap)/\SetupShareCap\) for a cap \(\SetupShareCap\), here 135 ms of work per call.

Systems insight: The ratio \(\CallUsefulWorkTime/\EnvelopeSetupTime\) determines the setup-cost trade-off. Calls with at least 135 ms of useful work can afford a fresh envelope each. Below that, retaining one envelope for the trajectory reduces overhead, at the price of a within-task trust policy, since state from earlier calls reaches later ones. Reuse never crosses tenants: ready capacity for the next task comes from pooling and verified reset (section 8.8). The comparison counts setup and useful work only, excluding model calls, queueing, and teardown.

8.5.6 Resource limits

Choosing an envelope and its lifetime settles what code can reach, not how much host capacity it may consume. An untrusted test script that enters an infinite loop, allocates gigabytes of heap, forks child processes in a tight loop, or streams gigabytes of logs to standard output can stall the host without ever breaching a file or network boundary. The host must therefore enforce resource quotas across the entire workload, including host-side helper threads and emulator processes, measured from the host kernel rather than from guest-reported counters. Even when hard quotas cap total usage, noisy-neighbor contention across shared CPU caches, memory channels, and storage buses requires separate monitoring, and validation suites should test runaway memory allocation and output floods alongside access-control denials.

On Linux systems, cgroups v2 provides a single control hierarchy to govern these execution trees across both container processes and microVM monitor and vCPU threads (The Linux Kernel Documentation Project 2024a). Within that hierarchy, cpu.max throttles the group once it exhausts its scheduling-period CPU quota, and pids.max caps process creation before a fork bomb can exhaust the host process table. Memory limits use two thresholds so the supervisor does not lose diagnostic state to an abrupt kill. Crossing memory.high throttles allocating threads and triggers synchronous page reclaim, whereas the out-of-memory (OOM) killer steps in only if usage reaches memory.max after reclaim fails. Because an unannounced OOM kill wipes out transient traces and in-memory state before the supervisor can read them, that graduated buffer gives the runtime room to record why the task stalled. Supervisors pair it with Pressure Stall Information (PSI) (The Linux Kernel Documentation Project 2024d), which tracks the share of elapsed time threads spend stalled on CPU runqueues, memory page allocation, or blocking disk I/O. By registering stall thresholds with epoll, the supervisor receives early warning when a sandbox enters severe contention and can trigger memory compaction, capture diagnostic state, or terminate the failing agent with an explicit reason code instead of losing the process silently.

The Linux Kernel Documentation Project. 2024a. Control Group V2. Linux kernel documentation.
The Linux Kernel Documentation Project. 2024d. PSI: Pressure Stall Information. Linux kernel documentation.

8.5.7 Accelerator and device access

Suppose the repository under repair contains compiled tensor kernels, so its test suite needs an accelerator to run. The candidate code the agent wrote now drives a physical device that another tenant will use next, and that device joins the isolation boundary. The envelope must then account for the host driver and the ioctl surface it exposes to the sandbox, and for the partitioning of compute and device memory. It must also account for faults that could crash a device shared with adjacent tenants, and for the device memory a tenant leaves behind, which the reset contract of section 8.8.1 must scrub. Offloading accelerator work to a remote service replaces direct hardware passthrough with a structured RPC interface, shifting the isolation burden to the service’s multi-tenancy and admission controls.

Direct hardware assignment exposes the host to device direct memory access (DMA), enabling peripheral devices to read and write arbitrary host physical addresses independently of CPU page tables. Standard CPU address-space isolation provides no protection against rogue DMA transactions. As detailed in the Linux VFIO framework (The Linux Kernel Documentation Project 2024e), secure device assignment requires an I/O Memory Management Unit (IOMMU) to translate and restrict device DMA requests through dedicated I/O page tables. Because the IOMMU sits upstream near the host root complex, it can police only transactions that travel all the way up the PCIe tree. If a downstream PCIe switch routes peer-to-peer traffic directly between two slots without access control services forcing that traffic up through the IOMMU, one device can read or overwrite its neighbor’s memory without translation. Devices that can reach each other along such paths share one IOMMU isolation group, so the true physical boundary often encompasses several slots rather than a single card. For a passed-through accelerator, the unit of isolation is therefore that group rather than the microVM. Every device in the group must be assigned to the same tenant, which means the group, not the guest, sets how finely a host can divide its accelerators among tenants. Hardware partitioning features that split a single physical accelerator into isolated instances narrow that sharing, yet they do not remove the surrounding obligations. The fault domain, the driver’s exposure, and the device memory left behind must still be validated, because CPU-level virtualization guarantees end at the PCIe bus.

The Linux Kernel Documentation Project. 2024e. VFIO: Virtual Function I/O. Linux kernel documentation.

The selected envelope establishes a rigorous execution boundary, resource policy, and lifecycle for compute. Multi-turn workflows, however, impose a parallel requirement for state continuity, because iterative test cycles require access to code modifications and intermediate artifacts generated across preceding turns. Decoupling this persistent filesystem state from the transient lifecycle of worker processes and sandboxes requires a dedicated task-local workspace architecture governed by explicit retention and export policies.

8.6 The Agent Workspace

In the connection-pool repair task, the agent generates an edit in one turn to create candidate patch r18, runs a shell command in the next turn to compile the code, and then invokes the test suite to produce an evaluation report. Spawning a fresh process for every command protects the host from runaway memory allocations or lingering background jobs, but those separate processes must still view and modify a shared, continuous filesystem state. Conversely, keeping a single process alive does not establish boundaries around which files the agent may alter. An agent execution environment therefore requires an explicit workspace policy independent of its execution envelope.

That workspace policy delineates four boundaries: the immutable source baseline, the task’s private modifications, temporary scratch space, and the artifacts eligible for final export. Generated commands execute strictly within a designated task workspace, isolated entirely from the host’s primary working tree or operational directories. For a code repair run, candidate edits and intermediate build artifacts reside inside this task workspace. At the same time, authoritative acceptance test suites, benchmark harnesses, and reporting mechanisms remain strictly outside the agent’s writable scope, even when the agent is permitted to write temporary test output into a monitored scratch directory.

8.6.1 Copy-on-write provisioning

A full private copy offers a straightforward way to provision an agent workspace, but creating duplicate copies of large source repositories, compilers, language runtimes, and dependencies introduces severe storage and I/O bottlenecks. Copy-on-write solves this by decoupling the immutable base environment from the modifications generated by individual tasks. The runtime pins a read-only base image and exposes a private, writable view for each trajectory. Read requests transparently access the shared base contents, while write operations allocate private storage on demand according to the underlying filesystem’s copying rules. Workspaces sharing the same underlying base remain completely isolated from each other’s mutations.

Copy-on-write provisioning moves what tasks write, not what they can see.

The Linux Kernel Documentation Project. 2024c. Overlay Filesystem. Linux kernel documentation.

A layered filesystem provides a standard implementation of this pattern at the file level. A read-only lower directory tree supplies the baseline repository, while a private, writable upper directory tree stores newly created, modified, or overwritten files. A union mount combines these two layers into a single merged namespace visible to the executing agent.
