When an agent deletes a file that exists in the read-only lower layer, the filesystem cannot remove the shared file; instead, it writes a special deletion marker into the upper layer to hide the lower entry. In Linux OverlayFS, this marker is known as a whiteout (The Linux Kernel Documentation Project 2024c). Because copy-up operations, directory merges, and whiteouts involve kernel metadata and filesystem-specific structures, the upper tree does not represent a clean, ready-to-commit source tree or patch.

The merged views in figure 5 distinguish shared bytes from task-visible files. The panels follow one overlay-filesystem backend while task A first edits a file and then deletes another; task B continues to read the baseline.

The private upper layer records differences from the baseline, including absence. Exporting the visible result therefore requires interpreting those differences; copying the upper directory alone would preserve internal deletion markers instead of a clean task artifact.

Furthermore, copy-on-write is a storage optimization, not an execution isolation boundary. Preventing concurrently executing tasks from modifying the shared base or reading each other’s private layers remains the duty of the sandbox execution envelope. That envelope also determines how the workspace is mounted and cached in memory. Containers sharing the host kernel can mount a file-level overlay directly, allowing every sandbox to read unmodified files from a single host page cache. A microVM, by contrast, typically attaches its workspace as a copy-on-write virtual block device managed by its own guest kernel. Unless the hypervisor maps shared host pages directly into the guest, each virtual machine caches the blocks it reads inside its own guest page cache, duplicating popular toolchain and library pages across active sandboxes.

8.6.2 Cost of the first modification

How much data copy-on-write actually copies on the first write depends on whether the storage layer tracks whole files or fixed-size blocks. Under a file-level overlay such as OverlayFS, modifying a single byte in a 1.2 GB test fixture forces the filesystem to copy the entire 1.2 GB file into the private upper layer before applying the write. Because copy-on-write defers copying from workspace creation until an agent first mutates a file, the granularity of that copy operation sets both first-write latency and storage overhead. A block-level storage volume or an extent-sharing filesystem instead allocates private storage only for the modified blocks or extents, leaving shared pointers in place for the rest of the file. Even block-level sharing cannot make a one-byte edit cost one physical byte of I/O, however, because metadata allocation, journaling, and page-cache updates still operate on whole blocks.

Napkin Math 8.2: Provisioning by copy, and the granularity of a first modification
Problem: An evaluation host starts 32 workspaces for the same task. The pinned base (repository, toolchain, and dependencies) is 2.4 GB, and each task writes about 40 MB of edits and build outputs. The workspaces share one local drive with a sequential rate of 7 GB/s. How much payload does a full private copy move compared with a copy-on-write view, and what does a later one-byte change to a shared file cost?

Provisioning: A full copy moves 32 × 2.4 GB = 76.8 GB before any agent runs, at least 11.0 s of drive time even at the sequential rate. A copy-on-write view starts as a mount, and only written data acquires private storage, 1.28 GB across all tasks, at least 0.18 s. The copy moves 60× as many bytes, and the ratio is the base size over the bytes each task writes. Copy-on-write makes provisioning cost track what the agent changes, not what it can see.

First modification: Each of 32 independent workspaces initially shares an unchanged 1.2 GB binary fixture. Each task overwrites 1 byte within that fixture. Compare two hypothetical implementations: the first copies the entire file’s payload into private storage before modifying it; the second copies only the one 64 KB block containing the update. Assume that block was shared before the update and that no other block needs copying. Neither implementation uses a copy offload or compression in this accounting.

Math: File-granularity copying moves 32 × 1.2 GB = 38.4 GB of payload. Under the stipulated block-copy rule, the corresponding total is 32 × 64 KB = 2,048 KB. Both support the same 32 bytes of requested logical changes, and the file-granularity payload is about 19,000× larger.

Systems insight: Two ratios set the storage bill. Provisioning cost scales with the base size over the bytes each task writes (60× here), so copy-on-write wins whenever tasks change a small fraction of what they can see and loses its advantage as that fraction approaches one. First-write cost scales with the fixture size over the copying unit (about 19,000× here), independent of the edit’s size, so large mutable fixtures belong on block-granular storage and large immutable ones stay read-only. The totals count payload only; metadata, journaling, and caching add traffic, so the drive-time figures are lower bounds.

These two ratios point to three storage rules:

  • Mount immutable fixtures read-only: Large test fixtures, model weights, and compiler binaries should remain strictly read-only whenever tasks do not require in-place mutation. Read-only mounts prevent inadvertent copy-up operations while allowing multiple concurrent sandboxes to share host page-cache entries.
  • Redirect volatile outputs to scratch volumes: Build outputs, compiler intermediate files, and execution logs should write directly to dedicated, bounded scratch storage rather than triggering copy-up chains within the primary workspace overlay.
  • Align storage granularity with access patterns: If tasks must perform localized edits on very large files or databases, systems should deploy block-level snapshotting or extent-sharing filesystems. Copy-on-write saves most I/O when an agent mutates a minor fraction of the workspace, but its performance advantage diminishes rapidly as modifications approach the size of the base image.

Even with copy-on-write efficiency, private task storage still needs hard bounds because runaway build logs, recursive file generation, or bloated cache directories can fill a host drive in seconds. The runtime therefore enforces quotas on both storage bytes and inode counts alongside I/O rate limits, surfacing any quota breach as an explicit resource-exhaustion error rather than letting a tool call fail with partial writes or corrupted state. Those per-workspace caps must also feed into host-level admission control, since dozens of sandboxes that each stay within a 10 GB quota can still exhaust a shared physical drive unless aggregate allocation is bounded.

8.6.3 Workspace leases

Across a multi-turn repair trajectory, intermediate build caches, compiler outputs, and source edits must survive from one tool call to the next without ever becoming visible to a concurrent or subsequent tenant. A workspace lease enforces this continuity by binding a provisioned workspace to a single task, owner, execution envelope, and lifetime. When the agent dispatches a command during its turn, the runtime attaches the worker process to the leased workspace; when a new task arrives, it receives a separate, freshly provisioned view.

↪ Checkpoints that bind the lease’s workspace files to a committed log position, so private state survives a worker crash as well as a turn boundary, are developed in Checkpoints.

The lease is an ownership and lifetime contract, not a concurrency lock. It differs from the scheduler’s lease of Operation identity and endpoint contracts, where a stale worker may still be running somewhere else and the destination must fence its writes with an epoch. A workspace is attached to one envelope, so a stale holder is fenced by the supervisor itself, which revokes the envelope’s access when the lease ends (section 8.8.1). Holding an active lease establishes which tenant owns a storage volume, but it does not guarantee that an earlier asynchronous command has finished writing or that orphaned background processes have closed their file handles. Before ending a tool turn or exporting workspace state, the runtime must stop lingering background jobs and flush pending I/O so that all writers are quiescent.

↩︎ Supervision and cancellation of background jobs that outlive a tool turn are established in Job supervision and cancellation.

When a task finishes, the runtime extracts only the outputs allowed by the task’s export policy before tearing down private state. For a code repair agent, those outputs typically include a unified patch against the pinned baseline, a cryptographic hash or manifest of the resulting source tree, and structured logs from the test suite. The export pipeline must read from a stable snapshot or wait until all writer processes have stopped, so that the exported patch matches the exact files the test suite ran against. It must also canonicalize and check every file path inside the workspace boundary; otherwise, a symbolic link planted by an untrusted agent could trick the exporter into reading and leaking a host file.

A runtime must never archive the raw upper directory of an overlay filesystem as the task deliverable, because that upper layer contains kernel whiteout markers, internal metadata, compiler caches, temporary build artifacts, and any secrets spilled into the workspace. Export pipelines instead parse and filter outputs into clean diffs and vetted test reports. Downstream release pipelines verify independently that a candidate patch applies cleanly to trunk and passes continuous integration under trusted credentials before merging. The runtime then discards the remaining private state under the pooling and reset rules of section 8.8. Controlling what the export pipeline extracts closes the file-based route out of the workspace. Running code inside the sandbox can, however, also open network sockets to send proprietary source code, test fixtures, or discovered vulnerabilities straight to an outside server, so the sandbox must govern outbound network traffic independently of storage.

8.7 Egress Control

Consider the threat model where credential brokering is in place. An untrusted script triggered by an injected README cannot read the host’s authentication token from guest memory, yet it still holds legitimate read access to the local source files it was asked to repair. If that script opens an outbound socket and sends the repository contents to an unapproved server, proprietary source code leaks without the attacker ever breaking the hypervisor boundary or stealing a cryptographic key. Egress control governs the communication channels leading outward from the sandbox, enforcing outbound limits on the host’s physical and virtual network path rather than trusting guest processes or client settings to cooperate.

8.7.1 Enforcement outside the guest

Setting HTTP_PROXY or HTTPS_PROXY inside the guest environment does nothing to stop an injected script that ignores environment variables, opens a raw socket to an external IP address, or tunnels data out over UDP. When a task evaluates self-contained code, runs local unit tests, or analyzes a static dataset, the cleanest defense is to give the sandbox no outbound network interface at all, leaving only a loopback device inside an isolated network namespace. Tasks that must pull packages, fetch remote Git commits, or report telemetry instead need a tightly scoped path through a host-owned service adapter or egress gateway. Host virtualization primitives such as Linux network namespaces, virtual Ethernet pairs, and nftables or eBPF cgroup filters block every other route outside the guest’s control boundary.

A complete network boundary covers direct IPv4 and IPv6 sockets, recursive DNS lookups, and host services reachable across local virtual bridges by combining two enforcement layers outside the guest. At the transport and network layer, a packet filter checks IP addresses and port numbers, admitting traffic only toward the designated mediator and dropping direct connections to raw IP literals or outside resolvers. At the application layer, the mediator inspects the protocol, service path, and operation. Testing this setup requires verifying that attempted bypasses, such as direct socket connections and unbrokered DNS queries, are dropped, not merely that cooperative HTTP clients work. Each layer covers the other’s blind spot (figure 6): the packet filter cannot distinguish a repository fetch from a push to the same IP address, while the application mediator never sees a raw socket connection that the packet filter has already dropped.

8.7.2 Destination, operation, and data scopes

Allowing an agent to reach a hostname is far coarser than allowing only the operation the task needs. A single code-hosting endpoint serves read-only repository clones, branch pushes, pull-request comments, and public release uploads under the same HTTPS address. Permitting the hostname github.com or an internal Git server without inspecting the request therefore lets an agent push commits or post private source files to a public issue thread. As table 6 shows, an egress policy must separate three questions: which destination endpoint the task may contact, which operation and resource it may touch there, and which data may travel in the request.

A host-owned service adapter enforces the first two scopes by mediating the API directly. When an agent asks to fetch a repository, the adapter checks the task’s identity, verifies that the target repository matches the task grant, and confirms that the requested operation is read-only before attaching the upstream credential on the host. The guest receives the fetched objects without ever seeing the token, while a request to push commits or read another repository on the same hostname is rejected at the adapter. The adapter must also validate query parameters and request fields so that a script cannot smuggle sensitive data out inside the URL parameters of a permitted GET request.

A standard HTTP forward proxy sees much less. When a proxy handles HTTPS traffic through an HTTP CONNECT tunnel, it relays an encrypted byte stream between the guest and the remote server. It sees only the initial target host and the Server Name Indication (SNI) header and remains blind to HTTP methods, URL paths, headers, and bodies. Enforcing operation- or payload-level rules through a proxy therefore requires terminating TLS at the gateway, decrypting and checking the request, and opening a separate upstream TLS connection. Because TLS interception requires installing a private root certificate authority inside the guest, which tools with pinned certificates or custom trust stores will reject, host-owned service adapters are more reliable when an operation grant must inspect the request.

8.7.3 Name resolution and local destinations

Inspecting the request at the gateway still fails if name resolution or routing can redirect an approved hostname to an internal target. If an egress gateway checks a hostname by resolving it once and then lets the guest open its own connection, an attacker-controlled DNS server can return an approved public IP address for the policy check. It can then switch the answer to 127.0.0.1 or an internal service address when the guest connects. To close this DNS rebinding window, the mediator must bind its approval to the exact IP address used for the connection and check every enabled address family including IPv6. It must also re-evaluate every HTTP redirect (301 or 302) before following it, so a public URL cannot redirect the request onto an internal host.

Host-local interfaces, private subnets (such as RFC 1918 ranges), and link-local addresses are the first targets of such redirects and direct probes. On cloud hosts, instances query the link-local metadata address 169.254.169.254 to fetch short-lived IAM credentials, hypervisor configuration, and bootstrap tokens without prior authentication. If packet filters block only public internet ranges and leave link-local or bridge addresses reachable, code inside the sandbox can read the host’s cloud role credentials in a single HTTP request. A network sandbox must therefore drop traffic to loopback, private, and link-local ranges by default and open only explicit host adapter endpoints.

Even when all direct TCP and UDP connections to the internet are blocked, an unrestricted recursive DNS resolver gives the guest an outbound data channel.8 A script inside the sandbox can hex- or base32-encode a secret into subdomain labels such as deadbeef.attacker.example and ask the recursive resolver to look the name up. The resolver then forwards that query to the attacker’s authoritative nameserver, delivering the secret across the perimeter. Rather than exposing a general recursive resolver to the guest, the runtime should resolve names inside the host adapter against a fixed allowlist of exact hostnames, never broad wildcard domains, which leave subdomain labels open for signaling. It should also pair any dynamic lookup path with rate limits and query logging.

8 The Confinement Problem: Lampson (1973) posed the problem of running a borrowed program so that it cannot leak its caller’s data, and observed that a program can signal through channels never intended for communication, such as its use of shared resources. Name lookups are such a channel for a guest whose web requests are blocked. Confinement has to account for every externally observable effect of the code, and a rate limit narrows a channel’s bandwidth without closing it.

Lampson, Butler W. 1973. “A Note on the Confinement Problem.” Communications of the ACM 16 (10): 613–15.

8.7.4 Disclosure through permitted channels

Even when packet filters, service adapters, destination allowlists, and operation grants all work as designed, sensitive data can still leave through a payload the task is allowed to send. The submit_report request of figure 2 contacts only the approved endpoint and calls only the permitted operation. Yet the injected instruction has tucked proprietary source code into the report body, so every sandbox boundary holds while the payload leaks data beyond the task’s scope. At the mediator, the runtime can narrow that channel by enforcing strict schemas that replace free-form text fields with typed key-value pairs and by capping request body sizes to stop bulk dumps. It can also scan outbound buffers for high-entropy strings, keys, and known token formats before forwarding the request. Pattern and entropy scanners remain heuristic, however, because paraphrased source code, compressed snippets, or custom encodings pass signature checks cleanly. Deciding whether data derived from a private file may flow into a permitted outbound call at all is an information-flow question, which Disclosure Through Permitted Tools develops for permitted tools.

Outbound network state also ends with the task lease. When a lease expires, the runtime must revoke its network identity and broker tokens at once, close active TCP connections, tear down virtual network interfaces, drop keep-alive sockets, and cancel in-flight brokered requests. Because a packet that has already left the host cannot be recalled by rolling back local disk state, preparing an execution environment for another tenant requires tearing down network and broker sessions alongside resetting compute and storage.

8.8 Sandbox Pools and Reset

The workspace lease keeps the repair’s edits, build artifacts, and helper processes alive across turns, and the egress controls bound what they can send. When the trajectory ends, a platform serving many tasks must provision the next tenant’s environment without delay. The next tenant must inherit none of the modified files, background daemons, open sockets, or credentials the repair left behind, yet it should not wait for a cold boot either. Sandbox pooling reconciles these requirements. The pool keeps a standby reserve of clean, pre-warmed instances to absorb arrivals, and an instance whose lease has ended rejoins the ready fleet only after a complete, verified reset, regardless of what status code its final tool returned.

Two naive extremes bracket this design space. At one end, a fresh envelope for every tool call carries no state between calls, but every call pays the full setup (Notebook 8.1 found setup to be 83.3 percent of each short parser call). The control plane must also pass the whole workspace across the boundary on every turn. At the other end, one long-lived environment reused across tasks pays no setup between trajectories, but mutable state accumulates in the guest, from stray files and corrupted configurations to backdoors planted by a prompt injection. A sandbox pool takes the middle path. It initializes instances in advance, holds them in a standby reserve, leases each instance to a single trajectory for that trajectory’s entire lifespan (section 8.6.3), and flags the instance as tainted the moment the lease expires. The tainted instance remains strictly unavailable until external hypervisor or operating system mechanisms either restore it to a verified baseline or destroy it entirely.

8.8.1 The reset contract

Wiping the workspace directory on disk does not make a used sandbox safe to hand to another tenant. A command run during the previous lease can double-fork a daemon that detaches from the task’s process group, or bind an abstract Unix domain socket with no filesystem path. It can plant a token in the kernel keyring, or schedule a cron job that fires minutes later. It can also shadow a binary earlier on the search path, or leave asynchronous work queued in a host broker. A reset contract specifies the exact conditions under which an execution environment is cleared for a new lease, enumerating every state domain that teardown or restoration must reset.

To stop cross-tenant leakage, the supervisor fences an expiring lease before cleanup begins, halting new tool calls under that lease and revoking its communication channels, credentials, and network authority. Next, the supervisor quiesces and terminates all owned execution, including child processes, background workers, and pending host-side tasks bound to the environment. A wall-clock timeout, error, or cancellation signal starts this sequence, but teardown requires positive confirmation from the enforcement layer rather than an elapsed timer. If the virtualization layer cannot confirm that all execution has stopped and that shared hardware resources have been severed cleanly, the supervisor destroys the instance instead of returning it to the pool.

Once all writers inside the sandbox are confirmed quiescent, the supervisor extracts and saves only the outputs permitted by the task’s export policy, such as the unified git diff of candidate edits and bounded test logs tied to the target commit. If artifacts are exported while the guest is still running, the supervisor enforces an explicit snapshot boundary so that concurrent writes cannot alter or corrupt the exported files. With permitted artifacts saved, the supervisor discards private mutable state or restores the environment from a trusted baseline, verifying readiness before assigning the sandbox to a new tenant. In the pool’s lifecycle (figure 7), the only path back to standby runs through that verification, and every other exit from reclaiming ends in destruction. Table 7 separates the required disposition across four state domains so a generic “reset complete” status cannot hide unhandled residue.

Destroying a used execution instance and building a replacement from a trusted baseline avoids brittle denylists of files, process names, or sockets. Restoring an existing instance in place can also satisfy the reset contract, provided the restore resets every mutable layer and severs external dependencies. The condition is needed because rolling back guest memory pages and CPU registers neither purges pending messages from a host broker queue nor reverses an external database write. Any external side effect emitted before the reset remains unsettled afterward.

The accelerator obligation deferred from section 8.5.7, scrubbing device state between tenants, lands here as the device row of table 7. Terminating guest processes leaves their allocations intact in accelerator memory, where a later tenant can read leftover activations or model weights through unzeroed allocations or through inter-process memory handles that outlive the process. Before the accelerator rejoins standby, the supervisor must therefore run a device-level reset it can confirm, such as a PCIe function-level reset or a driver-mediated memory scrub, that zeroes device memory, invalidates the device’s page tables, and severs shared handles. A device whose reset cannot be confirmed stays out of service, exactly like a guest whose teardown cannot be verified.

An asymmetry governs reset verification: finding a single leftover process or file proves that reset failed, whereas scanning for known residue and finding nothing cannot prove that every mutable object was cleared. Readiness checks must therefore verify that the restoration mechanism itself ran to completion, rather than inspecting the interior of the guest against a checklist after the fact. Any failed check or incomplete teardown sends the instance straight to quarantine and destruction.

8.8.2 Provisioning from a trusted baseline

Cold-booting a fresh sandbox on every task arrival forces the incoming task to wait several seconds while the monitor starts, the guest kernel boots, language runtimes initialize, and heavy libraries load into memory. Snapshotting removes that cold-boot wait from the admission path. The runtime builds a golden environment with the required operating-system packages, interpreters, compilers, and project dependencies, initializes it once, and captures the virtual machine’s memory, processor registers, and virtual device states into a trusted baseline snapshot. Arriving tasks then restore clean instances directly from that snapshot in milliseconds. The golden baseline must never include a prior tenant’s private files, active credentials, or task residue, and it must be rebuilt regularly as security patches, compiler updates, and library revisions age out older images.

What a snapshot covers depends on the virtualization mechanism. Ephemeral storage, virtual device queues, network sockets, message broker state, system clocks, and hardware entropy pools need separate initialization outside memory restore. When cloning a running guest image, the supervisor must never duplicate a live tenant identity or reuse restored pseudorandom number generator (PRNG) state across instances, which would cause two guests to generate identical cryptographic keys or nonces. Before handing a lease to an incoming agent and letting untrusted code run its first instruction, the supervisor binds a fresh tenant identity, reseeds entropy, mounts the private workspace, configures an isolated network namespace, and installs resource limits.

Mapping the snapshot’s memory file into each new virtual machine as private copy-on-write pages (mmap with MAP_PRIVATE) lets the entire pool share one physical copy of unwritten pages such as guest kernel text, shared libraries, and precompiled bytecode. Only when an instance writes to a page does the CPU take a write fault and allocate a private page in host RAM.9 Even so, a pool’s memory budget must count both shared and private allocations. Hypervisor control blocks, extended page tables, writable workspace pages, and virtual device state all consume private host RAM, so the snapshot file size on disk does not measure the active pool’s resident memory footprint.

9 Content-Dependent Memory Sharing: Sharing a selected read-only baseline differs from dynamically merging private pages because their contents happen to match, the content-based page sharing introduced for hypervisor memory management (Waldspurger 2002). With cross-tenant merging, the copy-on-write delay on a write can reveal that a matching page exists in another guest, a timing channel that was shown to detect which applications and files a neighboring virtual machine had loaded (Suzaki et al. 2011). A pool’s memory budget must therefore state which pages may be shared; baseline sharing is not a general guarantee against timing channels.

Waldspurger, Carl A. 2002. “Memory Resource Management in VMware ESX Server.” Proceedings of the 5th USENIX Symposium on Operating Systems Design and Implementation (OSDI 02), 181–94. https://doi.org/10.1145/844128.844146.
Suzaki, Kuniyasu, Kengo Iijima, Toshiki Yagi, and Cyrille Artho. 2011. “Memory Deduplication as a Threat to the Guest OS.” Proceedings of the Fourth European Workshop on System Security (EuroSec), 1–6. https://doi.org/10.1145/1972551.1972552.

10 Lazy Snapshot Restore: A study of serverless functions restored from microVM snapshots attributed their high startup latency to page faults that bring guest memory in from disk one page at a time (Ustiugov et al. 2021). Recording the stable working set of pages a function touches after restore and prefetching it in one pass substantially reduced that latency. For an agent pool, the analogous working set is the interpreter, toolchain, and dependency pages the first tool call touches, and its size, not the snapshot’s, sets the first command’s latency.

Ustiugov, Dmitrii, Plamen Petrov, Marios Kogias, Edouard Bugnion, and Boris Grot. 2021. “Benchmarking, Analysis, and Optimization of Serverless Function Snapshots.” Proceedings of the 26th ACM International Conference on Architectural Support for Programming Languages and Operating Systems (ASPLOS), 559–72. https://doi.org/10.1145/3445814.3446714.

Lazy restoration defers reading snapshot pages into RAM until the guest touches them.10 While lazy paging makes sandbox creation look instantaneous in admission logs, it pushes a storm of disk or network page faults into the agent’s first tool call, especially when a burst of newly admitted instances fault in their working sets at the same time. An operator must therefore measure both the time to return a lease handle and the time until the first tool command finishes useful work.

8.8.3 Sizing the standby pool for bursts

Even a fast snapshot restore takes tens of milliseconds, so a burst of tasks arriving together will queue behind restoration workers unless clean instances are already waiting in memory. To absorb arrivals without restore delays, a pool maintains a standby reserve of clean, unassigned instances. Admitting a trajectory consumes one standby instance and binds it to the task. The provisioning engine starts a replacement restore, and expired leases stay quarantined until they complete their reset contract. Because reserve math depends on how the pool schedules work under load, the pool’s policy must specify upfront whether waiting tasks take precedence over rebuilding standby. It must also specify how many parallel restores the host can run without saturating memory bandwidth, and how admission behaves when host memory or CPU is full.

Sizing the pool spans two timescales: long-run active leases and short-run burst replenishment. Little’s law (theorem 5.1) governs the long-run active count. Under a steady flow with admitted arrival rate \(\ArrivalRate\) and mean lease holding duration \(\LeaseHoldTime\), the average number of active sandbox leases is

\[ \ActiveLeaseCount = \ArrivalRate \, \LeaseHoldTime \tag{4}\]

↩︎ Little’s law, and the residency tax a tool wait imposes on retained attention state, are established in Residence time and service rate.

Because an agent holds its sandbox lease across the entire multi-turn trajectory rather than a single tool call, the agent’s deliberative horizon sets \(\LeaseHoldTime\): the sandbox stays allocated while the model samples tokens, waits in inference queues, and runs tests. This is the mirror image of the residency tax that retained attention state pays during a tool wait. There the attention state sits idle while a test runs, and here the sandbox sits idle while the model deliberates, so the same long horizon inflates both resident footprints. Little’s law gives the mean active count, but it neither bounds peak concurrency nor guarantees that a newly arrived task will find a warm instance ready. Variable trajectory lengths, sudden arrival bursts, and reset delays still require hard admission caps paired with queueing or backpressure.

The standby reserve covers the short timescale: the replenishment window \(\InstanceReplenishTime\) needed to prepare one clean instance. A cold boot pays monitor startup, guest kernel boot, and user-space setup in sequence (\(\SandboxColdBootTime = \MonitorStartupTime + \GuestKernelBootTime + \UserSpaceSetupTime\)), where starting language runtimes, importing libraries, and warming caches (\(\UserSpaceSetupTime\)) takes seconds. Restoring from a snapshot replaces kernel boot and user-space setup with restoring memory mappings, registers, and device state, cutting \(\InstanceReplenishTime\) to milliseconds (15 ms in the chapter’s illustrative profile). Snapshotting also reshapes memory-bus traffic during a burst. Let \(\GuestImageMemory\) be the full memory image of one guest and \(\LeaseDirtyMemory\) the private pages one instance actually writes. Cold-booting \(\BurstArrivalCount\) instances populates \(\BurstArrivalCount \GuestImageMemory\) bytes within \(\SandboxColdBootTime\), whereas \(\BurstArrivalCount\) restored instances allocate only their \(\BurstArrivalCount \LeaseDirtyMemory\) private pages and spread those writes across the trajectory.

The reserve estimate here uses an idealized replenishment policy. Offered requests form a stationary Poisson stream. Every offered request, including one that must wait, immediately orders one clean replacement that becomes ready after the same \(\InstanceReplenishTime\). Restore concurrency is unlimited, and active leases and memory have no admission limit. No request is lost, and completed replacements serve waiting requests in arrival order before rebuilding standby, so admitted throughput equals offered throughput and the same \(\ArrivalRate\) serves the active-lease estimate. Ended leases supply no ready instances until their reset completes and are not counted again as replenishment.

Under this policy, every arrival reduces net ready inventory (ready standby instances minus waiting requests) by one and starts one replacement that finishes after \(\InstanceReplenishTime\). The sum of net ready inventory and in-flight replacements therefore stays fixed at \(\StandbyReserveCount\). Just before a new request arrives, the replacements still in flight are the orders placed by earlier arrivals during the preceding \(\InstanceReplenishTime\) window. When that in-flight count is at least \(\StandbyReserveCount\), no ready instance remains in standby and the arriving request must wait. With one standby instance, for example, a second request that arrives before the first replacement finishes already waits. Because requests arrive as a Poisson stream at rate \(\ArrivalRate\), the number of arrivals during any window of duration \(\InstanceReplenishTime\) follows a Poisson distribution with mean \(\ArrivalRate \InstanceReplenishTime\) and variance \(\ArrivalRate \InstanceReplenishTime\). Because Poisson arrivals see the stationary state, the request-level miss probability is the Poisson tail at or above \(\StandbyReserveCount\). Lost requests, replenishment only for admitted requests, finite restore workers, variable lead times, and resource admission limits each require a different calculation. To screen a reserve against a miss target \(\StockoutProbabilityTarget\) (chosen by the operator, unrelated to the per-turn error rate of horizon compounding), approximating that Poisson count by a normal distribution with mean \(\ArrivalRate \InstanceReplenishTime\) and standard deviation \(\sqrt{\ArrivalRate \InstanceReplenishTime}\) yields

\[ \StandbyReserveCount \ge \ArrivalRate \InstanceReplenishTime + \StockoutNormalQuantile\sqrt{\ArrivalRate \InstanceReplenishTime} \tag{5}\]

where \(\StockoutNormalQuantile\) denotes the standard normal quantile.11 Round this screening estimate upward, then check the discrete Poisson tail at or above the chosen reserve, since the quantile expression is not an exact guarantee. At small means the normal approximation is unsuitable. A guest-page memory budget follows from copy-on-write page sharing across one golden template, private dirty pages accumulated by active leases, and base overheads for standby instances:

11 Square-Root Staffing: The reserve rule has the form of the square-root staffing rule for many-server queues, which provisions the mean load plus a margin proportional to its square root. Halfin and Whitt (1981) showed that holding the margin’s coefficient fixed keeps the probability of waiting at a stable value strictly between zero and one as the offered load grows. The relative margin shrinks as \(1/\sqrt{\ArrivalRate \InstanceReplenishTime}\). That resemblance motivates a sizing screen; the many-server waiting theorem does not establish a miss guarantee for this replenishment policy.

Halfin, Shlomo, and Ward Whitt. 1981. “Heavy-Traffic Limits for Queues with Many Exponential Servers.” Operations Research 29 (3): 567–88. https://doi.org/10.1287/opre.29.3.567.

\[ \PoolResidentMemory = \TemplateSharedMemory + \ActiveLeaseCount \, \LeaseDirtyMemory + \StandbyReserveCount \, \StandbyInstanceMemory \tag{6}\]

The equation combines mean active guest pages with the configured standby allowance; it is not an instantaneous bound. It excludes waiting demand, transient restores, quarantine, and host monitor, page-table, and control state. A real budget also sets aside headroom; Notebook 8.4 charges both explicitly. Notebook 8.3 applies these sizing relationships to a steady arrival stream.

Napkin Math 8.3: Sizing a pool from its arrival rate
Problem: A platform receives 2 offered coding trajectories per second under the no-loss, constant-lead-time policy, and each holds its lease for 300 s. In the chapter’s illustrative sandbox profile, restoring one clean instance from its snapshot takes 15 ms and a full guest image is 512 MB. Each active lease writes about 64 MB of private pages, and each standby keeps 8 MB. Does one standby meet the request-level miss target of 0.1 percent, what is the smallest supported reserve, and what guest-page allowance covers mean active leases and that reserve?

Math: By equation 4, active leases average \(\ArrivalRate \LeaseHoldTime\) = 2 per second × 300 s = 600. Arrivals during one restore average \(\ArrivalRate \InstanceReplenishTime\) = 0.03, far too small a mean for the normal approximation in equation 5, so the Poisson tail is read directly. One standby misses when at least one prior replacement remains outstanding, with probability 2.96 percent, above the target. The smallest supported reserve is 2; its miss event requires at least two prior outstanding replacements, with probability \(1 - e^{-\ArrivalRate \InstanceReplenishTime}(1 + \ArrivalRate \InstanceReplenishTime) \approx\) 0.044 percent, below the target. Private guest images for every active lease would occupy about 322.1 GB. With copy-on-write, equation 6 gives about 39.0 GB, 8.3× less.

Systems insight: The agent’s trajectory horizon, rather than the latency of individual tool calls, dictates overall fleet cost. Active leases and their resident memory grow linearly in \(\LeaseHoldTime\), so doubling trajectory length doubles the memory term, while copy-on-write sharing keeps a fleet of per-trajectory microVMs affordable. The reserve is governed instead by \(\ArrivalRate \InstanceReplenishTime\) and is sharply sensitive to it. The selected reserve of 2 meets the target only while restore stays below about 22 ms, and a 10× slower cold boot (\(\ArrivalRate \InstanceReplenishTime\) = 0.3) needs a reserve of 4. Because every attempt holds its sandbox for \(\LeaseHoldTime\), sandbox residency is part of \(\mathbb{E}[\AttemptCost]\) in the cost per accepted task \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\) (equation 1.2), and a rejected attempt pays it again. The estimate assumes the declared no-loss replenishment policy with constant lead time and unlimited restore concurrency; the next notebook treats a finite-worker burst.

Two standby instances meet the request-level target only while restore stays fast.

While steady Poisson arrivals provide a baseline for continuous operations, agent workloads frequently experience sudden synchronized surges, such as a continuous-integration suite launching parallel repair tasks. A burst also breaks an assumption behind equation 5, because a host can run only a fixed number of restores at once, so replenishments queue instead of all finishing within \(\InstanceReplenishTime\). Notebook 8.4 analyzes a deterministic burst under that limit to trace standby consumption, queue draining, and memory budgeting when arrival volume temporarily outstrips replenishment capacity.

Napkin Math 8.4: Consuming and replenishing a ready reserve
Problem: How long does a specified burst wait for clean environments, and what resident-memory allowance supports the resulting active leases and rebuilt reserve?

Variables: A hypothetical pool starts with 4 clean standby instances and no active leases. At the initial instant, 12 tasks arrive together. The supervisor admits as many as standby permits, then prioritizes queued tasks over rebuilding the reserve. It can restore 2 instances concurrently, each taking exactly 15 ms to become ready under that concurrency. Assume sufficient execution slots for all tasks, no other arrivals, no lease returns during the calculation, and no restore failures. Handoff and dispatch time are negligible in this model; the restore interval includes the required readiness work.

Math: Initial admission consumes the reserve, leaving 12 − 4 = 8 tasks waiting. The workers serve those tasks in 4 rounds of 15 ms, so the last admission occurs at 60 ms. Rebuilding the original reserve takes another 2 rounds. The pool has its full reserve again at 90 ms, while all 12 admitted leases remain active. Admitting the burst finishes allocating the environments, but the trajectories themselves continue executing.

For an illustrative memory allowance at that final state, suppose immutable resident pages consume 1,000 MB shared across the pool. Each active instance has 64 MB of additional private resident state, and each standby has 8 MB. The resulting 16 instances each require 8 MB for monitor, page-table, and control state. Reserve another 256 MB of headroom. The budget is in decimal megabytes and sums the exact byte quantities.

1,000 MB shared + 768 MB active-private + 32 MB standby-private + 128 MB control + 256 MB headroom = 2,184 MB.

The private-state terms include resident writable workspace pages, so the workspace is charged once. Host and broker services, storage caches, disk, and accelerator memory sit outside this pool-residency allowance. Transient restore buffers must fit within the stated headroom, and enforcement uses exact byte counts.

Systems insight: The standby reserve governs instantaneous admission capacity, while restoration concurrency dictates queue drainage rate. The last admission waits \(\lceil (\BurstArrivalCount - \StandbyReserveCount)/\RestoreWorkerCount \rceil \, \InstanceReplenishTime\) for a burst of \(\BurstArrivalCount\) tasks, a reserve of \(\StandbyReserveCount\), and \(\RestoreWorkerCount\) restore workers. Using 4 workers instead of 2 therefore cuts the wait from 60 ms to 30 ms, provided restores do not slow under the added concurrency. Each added standby admits one more task immediately and costs 8 MB of idle private memory. The trace is deterministic; a different arrival pattern needs its own calculation.

An exhausted reserve must trigger explicit queueing, deferral, or rejection signals; it must never license the supervisor to lease an unreset or unverified sandbox to bypass admission bottlenecks. Admission control must also track physical resource headroom across active execution and background restoration. Instances awaiting teardown or memory scrubbing continue to occupy host memory and hypervisor tracking tables without providing usable standby capacity. Delays in resetting expired leases therefore represent hidden capacity drains that operators must measure alongside restore latency and trajectory duration.

Checkpoint 8.2: Sizing and reclaiming a pool

The pool equations and the reset contract answer each of these.

8.8.4 Sandboxes for evaluation and training rollouts

↪ Repeated runs, confidence intervals, and paired comparisons for agents evaluated on this substrate are developed in Statistical Evaluation Rigor.

Jimenez, Carlos E., John Yang, Alexander Wettig, Shunyu Yao, Kexin Pei, Ofir Press, and Karthik Narasimhan. 2024. “SWE-Bench: Can Language Models Resolve Real-World GitHub Issues?” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).

When an evaluation harness or a reinforcement-learning rollout loop runs thousands of candidate patches against a repository’s native test suite, any leftover file from a prior attempt or any writable test script lets a candidate pass by corrupting the grader rather than fixing the bug. Benchmarks such as SWE-bench (Jimenez et al. 2024) and training rollout pools therefore rely on the same pooled sandbox lifecycle as production traffic. A pinned baseline snapshot and a verified reset prevent cross-attempt contamination, while read-only mounts keep the test harness outside the agent’s writable scope and leave the runner only bounded scratch storage and controlled logging channels. Even so, an evaluation team must separate execution containment from test quality. A clean sandbox guarantees that the test suite started from a known baseline. It does not guarantee that the tests cover the bug, or that external dependencies, non-deterministic timers, and floating-point drift will produce identical scores across runs. Whether the pool serves production, evaluation, or training, its guarantee has the same shape. It provides a contained execution with task-local continuity starting from a verified baseline. What happens inside that boundary, including which permitted channels the code uses and what it sends through them, lies outside that guarantee. A sandbox that passes every reset check can therefore still host a task that discloses what it was allowed to read.

8.9 Fallacies and Pitfalls

Robust containment requires both a sound enforcement boundary and sufficient host capacity to sustain that boundary under load. When deploying agent sandboxes, engineering teams often confuse practical filtering heuristics with architectural guarantees, or mistake microbenchmark startup latencies for the true operational cost of isolation.

Fallacy: Removing dangerous names removes the process’s authority.

Stripping dangerous symbols from an execution namespace hides convenient entry points, but it leaves the underlying runtime authority intact (section 8.3). Reachable object graphs, open file descriptors, and native extensions remain within the interpreter’s shared address space. For example, a caller can traverse an ordinary reference like total.__globals__ to retrieve the supervisor’s unredacted module namespace from an allowed helper function. Rice’s theorem rules out a sound and complete semantic classifier for unrestricted programs; it does not cause this reachable-reference escape or prohibit conservative safe subsets. While a specialized, deliberately restricted language can enforce safety invariants through its own formal semantics, executing untrusted code inside the supervisor’s process forces both into a single failure domain. True containment and resource limits must be enforced by the host operating system outside that interpreter process.

Pitfall: Choosing an envelope from a platform label alone.

Architectural labels like “container” or “microVM” specify virtualization mechanisms, not actual security boundaries (section 8.5). While a container shares the host kernel and a microVM interposes a guest kernel beneath a hypervisor monitor, the true blast radius depends on how the runtime environment is configured: filesystem mounts, virtualized devices, egress network routes, control groups, and trusted helper daemons. The same user-space kernel changed its trusted file-access path between releases without changing its name (section 8.5.1), and a microVM given a writable host mount or an unfiltered network route exposes exactly what its label suggests it hides. Systems engineers must evaluate the complete execution configuration against specific threat models and operational workloads. Lower startup latency indicates a lighter initialization path, not stronger isolation.

Fallacy: The cost of strong isolation is its startup latency.

Snapshot restoration reduces guest startup overhead to milliseconds, 15 ms in the chapter’s illustrative profile. However, boot latency is rarely the dominant infrastructure expense. The primary cost of sandboxing stems from cumulative memory residency across the lease horizon. At an arrival rate of 2 trajectories per second and lease durations of 300 s, the pool averages 600 active leases. Even with copy-on-write page sharing, mean active guest pages plus the configured reserve occupy roughly 39.0 GB, compared to the 322.1 GB required by isolated private images (Notebook 8.3). Sandbox provisioning must be budgeted by memory-seconds per trajectory, a quantity that scales directly with the lease hold time \(\LeaseHoldTime\), rather than evaluated solely by boot time.

Pitfall: Treating an allowed destination as approval of everything sent there.

A network egress rule restricts traffic to approved remote endpoints, but it cannot verify whether the payload in a request body is safe (table 6). Credential brokering keeps long-lived tokens and secret keys outside the guest environment while still allowing sandboxed code to request operations against authorized services. Under this design, an agent can use an approved outbound channel to exfiltrate private task data or modify remote state without ever extracting a credential or escaping the sandbox envelope. Preventing this residual abuse is an information-flow and semantic authorization challenge, rather than a perimeter-bypass flaw in the network sandbox.

Fallacy: An empty workspace proves that a used envelope is ready for another tenant.

Files represent only one of the four state domains cataloged in table 7. Even if a cleanup script successfully wipes every file, lingering processes from double-forked daemons, abstract Unix domain sockets, kernel keyring credentials, or modified environment search paths continue persisting. Any leftover artifact across these state domains compromises the next tenant. Multi-tenant recycling demands an explicit reset contract that accounts for every state domain an incoming lease could inspect, destroys or reinitializes that state to a known golden baseline, and validates isolation before reassignment. Moreover, no local cleanup protocol can undo external side effects already committed to remote APIs during the lease.

Pitfall: Sizing the standby reserve from a restore time measured on an idle host.

Determining standby pool capacity from initialization benchmarks gathered on an unloaded system severely underestimates provisioning requirements. Reserve sizing is governed by the traffic intensity parameter \(\ArrivalRate \InstanceReplenishTime\), where the request-level miss probability under the stated replenishment policy follows a steep Poisson tail. In the chapter’s illustrative profile, the selected reserve of 2 instances satisfies a 0.1 percent request-level miss objective only as long as snapshot restore completes in under 22 ms. If initialization degrades by a factor of 10×, as an unoptimized cold boot can, the required reserve jumps to 4 instances. Under burst traffic, concurrent VM restoration, host cache contention, and lazy demand paging across copy-on-write regions degrade \(\InstanceReplenishTime\) precisely when capacity is most critical. Sizing calculations must measure initialization latency under the target concurrency of the anticipated burst rather than in quiescent conditions (Notebook 8.4).

8.10 Summary

A sandbox is an enforceable contract defining the boundaries of execution. It specifies precisely which hardware and operating system resources untrusted code can touch, which platform components form the trusted computing base, and how long both the execution environment and its delegated privileges endure. Because these limits are enforced beneath the executing process, the boundary never depends on model compliance. However, strong isolation introduces concrete operational costs: every admitted trajectory incurs startup latency, memory footprint, and environment reset overhead. In the chapter’s README attack scenario, containment succeeds through architectural enforcement rather than prompt adherence. The injected command cannot read the host’s credentials or open a route to an unapproved destination, although the same instructions can still misuse a channel the task was permitted, as the submit_report request showed.

In the connection-pool repair scenario, this architecture preserves candidate patches and test artifacts while denying the agent access to the host account or the mutable state of concurrent tasks. The evaluation harness keeps validation suites outside the writable workspace, giving the test runner bounded scratch storage. This separation protects the integrity of gathered evidence, even though accepting the repair requires end-to-end evaluation beyond the targeted regression checks run against candidate r18. Table 8 summarizes the mathematical foundations established across the chapter, connecting access authorization and boundary overhead to the queueing models that govern warm-pool capacity.

Key Takeaways: Containment includes reach and lifetime
  • Execution environments define blast radius: Tool declarations specify intended interfaces, but the execution environment determines the files, sockets, and memory addresses a process can actually touch. The blast radius of an agent action spans everything its running code can read, mutate, exfiltrate, or consume. Consequently, robust isolation must be enforced by the operating system or hypervisor beneath the process.
  • In-process filtering cannot guarantee isolation: Code executing inside the supervisor’s runtime can reach the supervisor’s objects through attribute traversal and subclass walks, and native libraries share its address space and identity. The shown escape follows from shared object reach. A process or virtual machine boundary can govern that reach, while a purpose-built language can instead bound its operands, operations, and cost by construction.
  • Capabilities bound reach to what the task was handed: A capability binds authority to an unforgeable reference with an enforced lifetime instead of to ambient user identity. Attenuation can narrow a grant but never widen it, and deriving a child leaves the parent usable until the runtime withdraws it. Brokers let code request credentialed operations without holding the secret.
  • Envelope selection balances isolation against performance: Containers, user-space kernels, microVMs, and bytecode runtimes place trust boundaries at different system layers and pay the system-call tax on different calls. A call-heavy build favors a guest kernel over an emulating one. Retaining an envelope for the trajectory amortizes setup when the within-task trust policy permits reuse. A policy that requires fresh state between calls can justify per-call setup even when it dominates useful work.
  • Permitted egress channels create exfiltration risks: Network policies restrict unapproved endpoints, but permitted traffic, including API requests, artifact uploads, and DNS queries, can still carry sensitive task state outward. Once transport-level isolation is in place, safeguarding sensitive data becomes an information-flow tracking challenge.
  • Trajectory duration dictates pool capacity: By Little’s law, active sandbox leases scale with arrival rate and total trajectory length rather than individual tool execution time. Copy-on-write memory sharing can reduce the resident footprint of concurrent agent workloads. Strict reset contracts must scrub compute, storage, and network state before an instance returns to the standby pool.

Enforcing containment beneath the model (principle 9) ensures that execution boundaries remain intact regardless of model instructions or prompt injection attempts. This boundary complements the typed action contract (principle 7), separating the admission of a tool request from the confinement of the code that fulfills it. Under invariant closure (principle 1), these guarantees must reside below the model, in the supervisor and the envelope it configures, rather than in prompting guidelines. Ultimately, any isolation boundary protects only the specific resources its enforcement mechanisms monitor and reset across successive tool invocations.

8.11 Historical Perspective and Further Reading

Running a stranger’s program on a machine that holds something valuable is the oldest problem in operating-system protection. In the 1960s, multiprogrammed mainframes forced systems designers to isolate mutually suspicious users sharing a single CPU and physical memory. Dennis and Van Horn addressed this challenge by introducing capabilities: unforgeable tokens that bundle the identity of an object with the explicit rights granted to operate on it (Dennis and Van Horn 1966). This design departed from ambient, identity-based permissions. Lampson’s access matrix (Lampson 1974) then placed capability lists and access control lists in one model. To enforce such policies, Anderson established the concept of the reference monitor, a compact, tamper-proof mechanism that must mediate every access request between a subject and an object (Anderson 1972). Saltzer and Schroeder subsequently synthesized these insights into an enduring canon of security design principles, establishing least privilege, complete mediation, and fail-safe defaults as non-negotiable engineering requirements (Saltzer and Schroeder 1975). These principles apply directly to tool execution in agent runtimes, where tool code must never inherit the broad ambient authority of the host environment. Furthermore, Lampson recognized early that even when direct access is barred, confinement can fail through subtle covert channels that no designer intentionally provisioned (Lampson 1973). Hardy’s confused deputy (Hardy 1988) later gave ambient authority’s characteristic failure its name, and it reappears whenever an agent invokes external tools using ambient platform credentials.

Dennis, Jack B., and Earl C. Van Horn. 1966. “Programming Semantics for Multiprogrammed Computations.” Communications of the ACM 9 (3): 143–55. https://doi.org/10.1145/365230.365252.
Anderson, James P. 1972. Computer Security Technology Planning Study. ESD-TR-73-51. Deputy for Command; Management Systems, Electronic Systems Division, Air Force Systems Command.
Kivity, Avi, Yaniv Kamay, Dor Laor, Uri Lublin, and Anthony Liguori. 2007. Kvm: The Linux Virtual Machine Monitor.
Kamp, Poul-Henning, and Robert N. M. Watson. 2000. Jails: Confining the Omnipotent Root.

Each isolation envelope examined in this chapter descends from decades of mechanism design. Popek and Goldberg (1974) established the formal requirements for efficient, recursive virtualization, proving that all sensitive processor instructions must trap when executed in an unprivileged mode so that a virtual machine monitor can intercept and emulate them. For decades, commodity architectures like x86 violated these conditions because certain sensitive instructions failed silently rather than trapping. Hardware vendors resolved this deficit only when Intel (VT-x) and AMD (AMD-V) introduced hardware-assisted virtualization extensions with dedicated guest execution modes (Uhlig et al. 2005). Building on these CPU extensions, KVM integrated hypervisor capabilities directly into the Linux kernel, turning guest virtual machines into standard schedulable threads (Kivity et al. 2007). Along a parallel track, systems researchers pursued in-process isolation to eliminate hardware context-switching overhead. Wahbe and colleagues introduced software fault isolation (SFI), rewriting untrusted binary instructions to enforce memory safety within a single address space (Wahbe et al. 1993), an approach that WebAssembly later standardized into a portable, verifiable bytecode (Haas et al. 2017). Between hardware virtualization and language runtimes lies the shared-kernel lineage. System-call interposition initially promised lightweight confinement for unmodified binaries (Goldberg et al. 1996). Garfinkel, however, systematically cataloged the traps and pitfalls of user-level mediation, demonstrating how time-of-check to time-of-use races and incomplete kernel semantics inevitably compromise user-space monitors (Garfinkel 2003), which pushed security mediation back into the kernel. Concurrently, FreeBSD Jails partitioned a single kernel among mutually untrusted tenants (Kamp and Watson 2000), establishing the shared-kernel foundation that Linux namespaces and control groups later expanded into modern container runtimes. Capsicum proved that capability discipline could be retrofitted onto standard UNIX processes by restricting system calls and mediating access exclusively through file descriptors (Watson et al. 2010).

Multi-tenant cloud platforms subsequently transformed isolation into an engineering economics problem. Running untrusted, short-lived functions at high multi-tenant density exposed the memory footprint and startup latencies of conventional hypervisors. This friction drove the development of lightweight virtual machines that could boot in tens of milliseconds, matching container agility while preserving hardware-level isolation (Manco et al. 2017). Firecracker pushed this optimization further by stripping away legacy PC devices such as ACPI tables, IDE controllers, and PCI buses in favor of a minimal virtio device model and a lean, memory-safe implementation tailored specifically for transient workloads (Agache et al. 2020). To eliminate guest boot overhead entirely, systems engineers elevated microVM snapshotting and state restoration into a precise latency-engineering discipline. They found that the page faults that bring a restored guest’s memory in from disk dominate its startup, and they cut those faults by prefetching the working set each function touches (Ustiugov et al. 2021).

Manco, Filipe, Costin Lupu, Florian Schmidt, Jose Mendes, Simon Kuenzer, Sumit Sati, Kenichi Yasukata, Costin Raiciu, and Felipe Huici. 2017. “My VM Is Lighter (and Safer) Than Your Container.” Proceedings of the 26th Symposium on Operating Systems Principles (SOSP), 218–33. https://doi.org/10.1145/3132747.3132763.
Greshake, Kai, Sahar Abdelnabi, Shailesh Mishra, Christoph Endres, Thorsten Holz, and Mario Fritz. 2023. “Not What You’ve Signed up for: Compromising Real-World LLM-Integrated Applications with Indirect Prompt Injection.” Proceedings of the 16th ACM Workshop on Artificial Intelligence and Security, 79–90. https://doi.org/10.1145/3605764.3623985.

Agent systems inherit this entire architectural foundation, but they overturn a core operational assumption. In conventional serverless platforms, an untrusted author uploads fixed code in advance, and the runtime executes that code across isolated, stateless invocations. In an agent runtime, by contrast, the untrusted author sits directly inside the execution loop: an autonomous model generates and executes code dynamically at each step of an iterative trajectory. This code is steered by external, untrusted content retrieved from web pages, databases, or third-party tools, content that an adversary can deliberately manipulate through indirect prompt injection (Greshake et al. 2023). Furthermore, while a serverless function discards its environment after a brief invocation, an agent maintains a mutable, stateful environment across an extended multi-step problem-solving sequence. This persistent lifecycle turns state isolation, residual-data scrubbing, and network egress control from occasional provisioning tasks into continuous operational requirements.

  • Saltzer and Schroeder (1975), The Protection of Information in Computer Systems. The classical foundation of security engineering. It defines the core principles of least privilege, complete mediation, economy of mechanism, and fail-safe defaults. Reading it today reveals how directly five-decade-old operating system principles govern modern agent sandbox design.
  • Lampson (1973), A Note on the Confinement Problem. A landmark three-page paper that defines confinement and formalizes how untrusted programs leak information across covert channels. This analysis provides the conceptual framework for modern egress filtering, rate limiting, and residual-state scrubbing in agent sandboxes.
  • Hardy (1988), The Confused Deputy. An influential case study demonstrating how ambient authority causes security boundaries to collapse when a privileged service executes tasks on behalf of an unprivileged requester. It remains the canonical justification for capability-based security, demonstrating why agent tools must receive scoped authorization tokens rather than ambient platform credentials.
  • Popek and Goldberg (1974), Formal Requirements for Virtualizable Third Generation Architectures. The foundational formalization of trap-and-emulate virtualization. It defines the precise conditions under which sensitive instructions must trap, establishing the theoretical guarantees that allow modern hypervisors and microVM boundaries to contain arbitrary guest code.
  • Garfinkel (2003), Traps and Pitfalls. An indispensable catalog of failure modes in user-level system-call mediation, analyzing race conditions, path aliasing, and the difficulty of tracking kernel state from user space. It remains an essential checklist for evaluating sandboxes built from ptrace or seccomp filters.
  • Agache et al. (2020), Firecracker. The production architecture of a minimalist microVM monitor built in Rust for dense multi-tenant workloads. Read it for the concrete engineering trade-offs among device surface, boot latency, and memory overhead that dictate how to size and manage an agent sandbox pool.
Saltzer, J. H., and M. D. Schroeder. 1975. “The Protection of Information in Computer Systems.” Proceedings of the IEEE 63 (9): 1278–308. https://doi.org/10.1109/proc.1975.9939.
Hardy, Norm. 1988. “The Confused Deputy: (Or Why Capabilities Might Have Been Invented).” ACM SIGOPS Operating Systems Review 22 (4): 36–38. https://doi.org/10.1145/60609.60611.
Agache, Alexandru, Marc Brooker, Andreea Florescu, Alexandra Iordache, Anthony Liguori, Rolf Neugebauer, Phil Piwonka, and Diana-Maria Popa. 2020. “Firecracker: Lightweight Virtualization for Serverless Applications.” Proceedings of the 17th USENIX Symposium on Networked Systems Design and Implementation (NSDI 20), 419–34.

8.12 Exercises

All givens below are hypothetical problem inputs, not measurements of any product. Decimal units apply throughout these problems (1 GB is \(10^9\) bytes and 1 KB is \(10^3\) bytes), while configured guest memory in the worked notebooks is a binary capacity quoted in conventional megabytes, so a problem’s answer need not reproduce a notebook value digit for digit.

Problem 1 (Basic): Splitting a repair grant across test shards. A repair task holds \(\RepairCapabilityGrant = (\GrantRights=\{\text{read}, \text{write}, \text{execute}\},\ \mathcal{O}_\CapabilityGrant=\texttt{repo/},\ \GrantCondition,\ \GrantExpiryTime=\GrantIssueTime_0 + 900\,\text{s})\), where \(\GrantCondition\) binds use to this task and carries an aggregate budget of 600 CPU-seconds. The controller proposes three children for parallel test shards: \(\TestsDirectoryShardGrant = (\{\text{read}, \text{execute}\},\ \texttt{repo/tests/},\ 250\ \text{CPU-s},\ \GrantIssueTime_0 + 600\,\text{s})\), \(\WholeRepositoryShardGrant = (\{\text{read}, \text{execute}\},\ \texttt{repo/},\ 200\ \text{CPU-s},\ \GrantIssueTime_0 + 1200\,\text{s})\), and \(\SourceWriteShardGrant = (\{\text{read}, \text{write}\},\ \texttt{repo/src/},\ 200\ \text{CPU-s},\ \GrantIssueTime_0 + 600\,\text{s})\). (a) Apply the component-wise attenuation check to each child and decide which may be issued. (b) The parent keeps 150 CPU-seconds for its own work. Decide whether the valid children fit the aggregate budget, and whether they would still fit if \(\WholeRepositoryShardGrant\) were corrected to expire at \(\GrantIssueTime_0 + 900\,\text{s}\). (c) At \(\GrantIssueTime_0 + 700\,\text{s}\) a shard holding \(\TestsDirectoryShardGrant\) requests execute on repo/tests/run.sh. Evaluate equation 1 term by term and state the verdict.

Problem 2 (Basic): Resident memory of a repair pool. A platform admits repair trajectories at \(\ArrivalRate = 0.25\ \text{s}^{-1}\), each holding its lease for \(\LeaseHoldTime = 1600\,\text{s}\). The shared template occupies 0.5 GB, each active lease writes 96 MB of private pages, three standby instances hold 16 MB each, and a full private guest image would be 0.5 GB. The pool host has 64 GB for sandbox residency. (a) Compute \(\ActiveLeaseCount\) and \(\PoolResidentMemory\) by equation 6, and compare with private images. (b) Trajectories double in length. Recompute \(\PoolResidentMemory\) and decide whether the host still fits. (c) If it does not, find the largest admission rate the host supports at the longer horizon and state the admission policy that enforces it.

Problem 3 (Basic): Paying the system-call tax on a build. An untrusted dependency build performs 30 s of useful computation and issues \(2 \times 10^{6}\) system calls, of which \(5 \times 10^{4}\) touch a virtual device. Take these per-call costs as givens. A shared-kernel container pays \(\PrivilegeTrapTime = 0.1\,\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\) and \(\SystemCallFilterTime = 0.05\,\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\) on every call. A user-space kernel pays \(0.1\,\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\) trap, \(1.5\,\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\) emulation, and \(2.0\,\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\) switch on every call. A microVM pays a \(0.1\,\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\) native trap on ordinary calls and \(5.1\,\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\) in total on each device call. (a) Using equation 3, compute each envelope’s added time and its overhead relative to the useful work. (b) Find the system-call count at which the user-space kernel’s overhead reaches 10 percent. (c) The build holds no credentials but runs code nobody reviewed. Choose the envelope and justify the choice by both cost and trusted components.

Problem 4 (Intermediate): Guard checks for the remediation agent. The remediation agent attaches a model-written guard to each proposed configuration change, such as p99_ms < 400 and error_rate < 0.01. The supervisor, which holds the agent’s production-change credentials, evaluates the guard on 500 service samples per second, with a 10 ms deadline per sample and a budget of 0.5 CPU cores for guard checks. Three options are available. The first is an in-process evaluator for a purpose-built expression language whose only operands are the supplied metric values and numeric literals. The language offers arithmetic, comparisons, and, and or, with no attribute access, subscripts, calls, or loops, and at most 200 syntax nodes at no more than 0.2 µs each. The others are a fresh bytecode sandbox per sample (0.5 ms setup) and a fresh microVM per sample (20 ms setup). (a) Compute each option’s worst-case time per sample and the CPU cores it consumes, and decide which options meet the deadline and the budget. (b) The agent asks for a matches(line, pattern) operator over log lines of up to 200 characters. A backtracking matcher can take \(2^\AdversarialInputLength\) steps of 10 ns on an adversarial \(\AdversarialInputLength\)-character input. A linear-time automaton matcher, with patterns capped at 100 states, takes at most one 10 ns step per state per character. Compute the backtracking time on a 40-character line and the longest input that stays within the evaluator’s 40 µs bound, then the linear matcher’s worst case and the resulting CPU cores. Decide which matcher, if either, may run inside the supervisor, and explain why the supervisor cannot simply stop a runaway match. (c) The agent next proposes replacing guards with a model-written Python script that parses the last minute of logs through a native parsing library, running once per minute with 2 s of useful work. Test the script against each condition that made the guard safe to evaluate in-process, choose its envelope and whether to start one per run under a 10 percent setup cap, and state which authority that envelope may hold.

Problem 5 (Intermediate): Per-document envelopes for the research agent. The research agent parses 400 retrieved pages while answering one question, each parse needing 2 ms of useful work and no filesystem or network access. The task requires mutable parser and module state from each independently hostile page to be inaccessible to the next; sharing a trusted read-only baseline is permitted. The options are a fresh bytecode sandbox per page (0.5 ms setup), a fresh microVM per page (20 ms setup), or one microVM retained for the whole question (20 ms setup once). (a) Compute the elapsed setup plus work for each option and each option’s setup share of a fresh call. (b) For a cap of \(\SetupShareCap = 10\) percent, compute the minimum per-call work for each fresh-per-call option. (c) The retained microVM is fastest. Apply the stated freshness requirement to decide whether the retained microVM is eligible, and decide which option to deploy even though it misses the 10 percent cap.

Problem 6 (Intermediate): Sizing the reserve under load, not at idle. A repair pool receives offered Poisson requests at \(\ArrivalRate = 0.5\ \text{s}^{-1}\) under the no-loss, constant-lead-time replenishment policy of section 8.8.3, with a request-level miss target \(\StockoutProbabilityTarget = 10^{-3}\). Snapshot restore takes 40 ms on an idle host but 200 ms at the restore concurrency a burst produces. (a) For each restore time, compute \(\ArrivalRate \InstanceReplenishTime\) and the smallest reserve whose Poisson tail at or above the reserve meets the target. (b) Find the largest \(\InstanceReplenishTime\) each of the reserves one and two tolerates. (c) Evaluate the normal approximation of equation 5 at the loaded mean, explain why its answer should not be trusted here, and decide the reserve to provision.

Problem 7 (Intermediate): Draining a burst of repair tasks. A continuous-integration trigger releases forty repair tasks at once into a pool holding eight standby instances. With up to four concurrent restore workers each restore takes 25 ms; with eight workers restores contend and each takes 40 ms. Queued tasks take precedence over rebuilding the reserve, and each standby holds 16 MB of private memory. (a) Compute the last admission’s wait and the time at which the reserve of eight is rebuilt with four workers. (b) The platform requires every task in such a burst to start within 100 ms. Decide whether doubling the workers meets the requirement. (c) Find the smallest reserve that meets it with four workers, its standing memory cost, and the time at which that larger reserve is rebuilt.

Problem 8 (Intermediate): When copy-on-write stops paying. An evaluation host starts sixty-four workspaces from a 6 GB pinned base; each task writes 150 MB of edits and build outputs, and the shared drive sustains 5 GB/s. (a) Compute provisioning payload and drive-time lower bounds for full private copies and for a copy-on-write view. (b) The test suite then modifies a 3 GB database fixture in place once per task, and the storage copies whole files on first write. Recompute the copy-on-write payload and its advantage over full copies. (c) Redesign the fixture’s storage so that the advantage returns, given that the tests touch 2,000 blocks of 64 KB. Compute the resulting payload, drive time, and advantage, and name one alternative that avoids copying altogether.

Problem 9 (Advanced): Admission latency for the support agent. The support agent runs each customer session in its own sandbox. At peak it receives offered Poisson sessions at \(\ArrivalRate = 40\ \text{s}^{-1}\) under the same no-loss, constant-lead-time replenishment policy, so eventual admitted throughput equals that rate. Each admitted lease lasts \(\LeaseHoldTime = 45\,\text{s}\), and a customer waiting for a sandbox counts against a 1 s first-response budget. Two provisioning paths exist: snapshot restore at 50 ms, or cold boot at 1.5 s. Use \(\StockoutProbabilityTarget = 10^{-3}\), a 0.5 GB template, 24 MB of private pages per active session, and 16 MB per standby. (a) Compute \(\ActiveLeaseCount\) and, for each path, \(\ArrivalRate \InstanceReplenishTime\), the reserve from equation 5, and the smallest reserve from the exact Poisson tail at or above the reserve. (b) Compute \(\PoolResidentMemory\) for each path and the relative margin \(\StockoutNormalQuantile\sqrt{\ArrivalRate \InstanceReplenishTime}/(\ArrivalRate \InstanceReplenishTime)\). (c) Compare with Problem 2. Identify which term binds the repair pool and which binds the support pool, and recommend a provisioning path and reserve conditional on the declared policy. State why the miss target alone does not prove the 1 s end-to-end response budget, and identify the remaining response-work and waiting-time evidence needed.

Problem 10 (Advanced): Critique a rate-limited resolver. A design review proposes letting repair guests resolve any name under the package mirror’s domain subtree, limited to 20 queries per second, and claims the limit “bounds exfiltration.” Each query name can carry 120 base32 characters of attacker-chosen labels (5 bits each), and a trajectory lasts 1200 s. A legitimate dependency install needs about 400 lookups. (a) Compute the channel’s payload per query, its rate at the limit, the time to leak a 40 KB file, and the total a trajectory could leak. (b) Compute the query budget per trajectory that would hold leakage below 1 KB, and compare it with the install’s legitimate lookups. (c) State what the rate limit actually bounds, then redesign name resolution so the guest’s lookups stop being a channel while installs still work, and say which residual channel the redesign leaves.

Problem 11 (Advanced): Suspend the sandbox while the model deliberates. Extend Problem 2 at the doubled horizon (\(\LeaseHoldTime = 3200\,\text{s}\)). Each trajectory runs forty turns, and 70 percent of every lease is spent waiting for model calls. A proposal suspends the guest to the shared 5 GB/s drive at the start of each model call and resumes it after, writing and then reading its 96 MB of private pages every turn; suspended guests hold no host memory. (a) Compute the admission rate the 64 GB host supports with suspension, the admission rate at which the drive saturates, and the resulting gain over Problem 2(c). (b) Compute the lower bound on added resume latency per turn and per trajectory, and choose an operating admission rate that leaves the drive 20 percent headroom for provisioning traffic. (c) The binding constraint has moved. Name it, and list the reset-contract and authority conditions a suspended guest must satisfy (which work may be running, what happens to its broker sessions, and where its image may go) before the proposal can ship.

What’s Next: From contained code to a defended agent
The submit_report request that passed every boundary in this chapter is not an isolated gap. The same README text can persuade the model to place a private test fixture in the query of a permitted package search. The query reaches an approved registry, the broker keeps its credential, and the code never leaves its envelope, yet the registry receives information the task was never authorized to share. The README can also claim that the test suite passed, and no envelope stops the agent from recording that claim as evidence. Agent Security opens with these attacks through permitted operations and builds the runtime checks that decide which data may cross an approved boundary and which sources may justify a consequential decision.
